Put to the Architect with three options (seventh kind / borrow / borrow-plus-allowlist) and answered: seventh kind. **SRS FR-11.1 was amended in the same change**, not after it, to name outbound public communication among the classes requiring approval. The addition is safe by construction and can only ever tighten: a gate policy that never mentions `outbound` has no rule for it, `strictestRuleFor` returns null, and `evaluateGate` holds — so every policy written before this kind existed now refuses outbound posting. Asserted by a mutation that collapses the kind back into `prod-facing`, which turns the profile suite red.
- 2026-08-31 · (M7.5) **The auto-post gate is a TYPE, not a guard** · the package's risk line says "auto-post" is the first outward-facing irreversible act the company can take on its own and that the gate belongs in the harness. A boolean parameter or an `if` would have satisfied that sentence literally and still been one careless caller away from a posted comment, so `GitHubHarbor.postComment` takes a branded `PostPermit` and nothing else — the `StationReason` idiom already in `src/shared/stations.ts`. It has exactly two constructors: `permitToPost`, which returns null for every disposition that is not `post` (i.e. every level below `autonomous`), and `permitFromApproval`, which returns null unless a gate verdict actually approved it. A draft-only flow therefore cannot write an expression that satisfies the poster's signature — the absence of a posting path is structural and is asserted on the API SURFACE, the S-SECRETS pattern applied to an outbound act rather than to a secret. `dispositionFor` is an exhaustive switch rather than a `>=` threshold for the same reason: adding an autonomy level should fail to compile, not silently land in whichever branch the inequality happened to cover.
- 2026-08-31 · (M7.5) **Batching into the standup IS the gate, not a digest beside it** · UC-10 step 3 says outbound comments above the configured level are "batched into the standup by default", and the obvious reading is a second queue the brief renders. It is the wrong one: two records of one pending approval can disagree, and the disagreement would be in the direction of a comment that looked approved in one place and was not in the other. Instead `supervised` opens an ordinary `outbound` gate, and `BriefInput.openGates` is already what the briefing reads — one record, seen from two angles. The WHOLE draft is handed to the gate rather than a summary of it, because UC-08's packaging is what the Architect actually reads when deciding and approving a comment without seeing its text is approving a signature on a blank page; the four packaging sections render from `prompts/watch/outbound-*.md` with the draft's facts interpolated (invariant §8).
- 2026-08-31 · (M7.5) **One endpoint, two filings; and an agent on no profile is draft-only** · outbound drafts arrive at `agent.harbor`, the address M7.4 already gave triage reports, dispatched on the subject the agent wrote — the ADR-0008 pattern the Odeon endpoint uses ("one address, three filings"), because the Harbor is one subsystem (everything in and out) and a second reserved id would put two harness identities where the design has one. An unrecognised subject is refused rather than guessed at. Separately: `outboundAutonomy` returning null means nobody put this agent on a profile, and that resolves to `manual`, not to the global default — an agent with no profile has no standing to speak publicly under the company's name, and the benefit of the doubt is the wrong way for this particular question to fail. Mutation-checked in both directions.
- 2026-08-31 · (M7.5, THE DOGFOOD CLAIM HELD A SECOND TIME) **The Front Office needed no profile-schema change** · ADR-0012's central claim survived a second built-in with a genuinely different shape — the Skeleton Crew is an incident path, the Front Office is a configurable outbound ladder — and both are three-to-four hires, triggers, playbooks, a memo policy and a harbor file in M7.1's frozen format. The one thing M7.5 did need was a gate KIND, which is the Watch's vocabulary rather than the bundle's, and which the Architect decided before any code was written. Worth stating precisely because the temptation was to widen the profile schema instead: a `replyAutonomy` field in `profile.json` would have solved the same problem by amending a public contract three packages after freezing it, and would have put the answer somewhere the Watch could not see it.

- 2026-08-31 · (M7.6) **The share envelope carries FILES, and the manifest is DERIVED rather than trusted** · two decisions shape the whole package. ADR-0012's argument for declarative bundles is that they are plain files, diffable in review — so an envelope carrying a re-serialized object would hand a reviewer a diff of the importer's formatting rather than of what the author wrote, and would round-trip losslessly only for the fields this build happens to know. Carrying the text makes export→import lossless by construction, asserted byte-for-byte. And the manifest — the disclosure the human reads before confirming — is RECOMPUTED from the payload on import by the same function that produced it on export, with any mismatch refused by name. That single check turns the confirmation from a decoration into a gate: without it the human approves a summary the payload is free to contradict. It is also what gives PROGRESS's "an undeclared env grant" a mechanical meaning — a hire quietly asking for a credential the summary did not list. The manifest declares EVERY gate class, including ones the bundle never mentions and which therefore take its default, because a manifest listing only the explicit overrides would let a permissive default arrive undisclosed.
- 2026-08-31 · (M7.6) **"Widened autonomy" means two things and both are checked** · activation-time clamping (`composeAutonomy`) already stops a profile exceeding the global ceiling, so an import-time check looks redundant at first. It is not: the sharper case is a shared bundle REUSING A NAME the Architect already trusts, arriving with more authority than the version it replaces. The global ceiling might legitimately be high; the point is that this profile's own request grew without anyone deciding it should. So the import also compares against what is installed under that name and refuses a raise of any class, or any new env grant. Narrowing is deliberately allowed — a shared bundle that tightens a profile is what a security fix looks like, and refusing it would push people to import under a new name and lose the history.
- 2026-08-31 · (M7.6, FOUR NEW IPC CHANNELS UNDER THE M3.1 RULE) **`inspect` and `install` are separate channels, and neither activates** · SDD §5's abridged list names `hireExport(role)` and `hireImport(blob)`; the shipped surface is four — `harbor:hire-export`, `harbor:profile-export`, `harbor:import-inspect`, `harbor:import-install` — with SDD §5 updated in the same change (the M3.1 rule: a new channel gets a doc line and a DECISIONS-LOG entry, or it does not ship). The split IS the requirement rather than a convenience: FR-10.4 says "import only pre-fills the spawn form — a human always confirms", so INSPECT reads a blob and returns a disclosure while writing nothing, and INSTALL is what a confirmed form reaches. There is deliberately no channel that does both and none that activates — an imported profile is inert until `profiles:activate`, its own Architect action, so there are two human steps rather than one. Asserted on the API surface the S-SECRETS way: the four channels are pinned, and `HireExchange` has no spawn, scheduler or activation seam on it at all, which is stronger than a flag saying not to. `inspect`'s purity is asserted by a CENSUS of the file tree before and after, because "it does not write" is exactly the sort of claim a later refactor breaks silently.
- 2026-08-31 · (M7.6) **One list of secret shapes, in two places, with drift made impossible to miss** · `scripts/check-invariants.cjs` has carried the credential patterns since M0 as a BUILD gate, and it is a standalone `.cjs` that must run before anything compiles — so it cannot import the new runtime module. Two copies of a security list is normally how a check quietly stops catching things: somebody adds a pattern to one side and the other keeps passing. `test/shared/secret-shapes.test.ts` reads the checker as TEXT and compares the lists element by element, so drift fails by name in the same commit that causes it; it also asserts the checker still declares the list in a shape the extractor can find, because silently finding nothing would make the suite pass forever against an empty list. The detector returns the credential's NAME and never the matched text — a refusal that quoted the secret would copy it into the log and any bug report the Architect pastes it into, turning a caught leak into a wider one. And it is honest about its reach: an unshaped password matches nothing, so the import path says "no known credential shape found" rather than "safe".
- 2026-08-31 · (M7.6, FIVE PRIVILEGE ESCALATIONS FOUND BY AN ADVERSARIAL PASS AGAINST MY OWN CODE) **The happy-path suite was green through all five** · a four-lens adversarial review ran against this module WHILE it was being written and found five real holes, each now fixed with a regression named for it. (1) **Path traversal:** the payload's record KEYS were bare `z.string()`, and `install` writes with `path.join`, which resolves `..` — so a playbook named `../../../gate-policy.json` wrote over the Watch's own policy file, which SDD §2 says "can only ever loosen, never tighten". A complete bypass of the approval system, found by a probe that planted the name and asserted the import SUCCEEDED. (2) **JSON escapes:** the secret scan read the raw blob text, so a credential spelled with unicode escape sequences matched nothing while `JSON.parse` handed the importer a real token; the scan now walks decoded values and keys. (3) **`install` merged instead of replacing:** a v2 that dropped a hire or trigger left the old one on disk and the loader read back the UNION, so the Architect could confirm two hires and get three with the third still armed. (4) **The widening check was skipped when the installed copy did not parse**, so a bundle arriving while the installed profile happened to be broken got MORE latitude than one arriving while it was healthy — the wrong way round; "we cannot tell what this name may do" is now a reason to stop. (5) **The manifest disclosed NAMES but not PROSE**, so a replacement with an identical name list could rewrite every runbook — and a playbook is the agent's task list, on a timer, with whatever autonomy the profile holds. Closed by a prose digest over every brief and playbook body. The general lesson is the M6 one at a new angle: a green suite proves the paths you thought of, and an adversary's first move is the path you did not.
- 2026-08-31 · (M7.6) **The prose digest is FNV-1a and is NOT a signature** · a 32-bit non-cryptographic hash over the sorted list of every hire brief and playbook body — sorted so a reordered bundle is not reported as changed prose, and separator-terminated so `["ab","c"]` and `["a","bc"]` cannot collide. Deliberately not SHA-256: the manifest is recomputed from the payload on import, so the digest authenticates nobody and resists no determined forger — its whole job is to make "the instructions are not the ones you approved last time" VISIBLE to a human comparing two imports. A crypto hash here would imply a guarantee this design does not make, and somebody would eventually rely on it. A digest rather than the text itself because the manifest is a disclosure a human reads, and pasting four runbooks into it would bury the grants and autonomy rows that also need reading; the files are plain and diffable (ADR-0012), so the diff is where the reading happens.
- 2026-08-31 · (M7.6) **Two mutations survived a first pass and both were FIXED rather than accepted** · the object-key branch of the secret scan and the install-side bare-name guard both survived, and each survival was diagnostic rather than cosmetic. The key-scan test asserted only that the import was refused — which the strict envelope schema does anyway for an unknown key — so the assertion could not distinguish the scan from the schema; it now asserts the REASON ("a GitHub token"), which is also the fact the Architect actually needs. The install-side guard was genuinely unreachable, because `profilePayloadSchema` refuses a traversing name first; rather than delete a defence-in-depth check on the one call that turns author-controlled text into a filesystem path, it was extracted to an exported `escapingNames` with its own test. That is the M6 lesson restated in miniature: a guard no test can reach is indistinguishable from one that is not there, and the fix is usually to give the logic a name rather than to delete it or to trust it.

- 2026-09-01 · (M7.7, A DEFECT THE EXIT REVIEW EXISTS TO CATCH) **The briefing compiler had no incident branch, so SRS §6.1's last clause was unreachable** · `compileFacts` read tasks, gates, memos, spend, breaker trips and the Gymnasium slice, and had never heard of the `incident-raised` / `incident-triaged` / `incident-announce-owed` entries the incident endpoint has written to `log.jsonl` since M7.4. An incident therefore reached the standup only sideways — as an open gate, or as whatever task Artemis happened to create — so the Architect was never told that something broke, in which repository, or what the on-call agent concluded. §6.1's "the next briefing narrates the incident accurately from the log" was unreachable BY CONSTRUCTION while every suite was green. VOICE-DESIGN §4 had specified it from the start ("Health — budgets vs burn, breaker trips, **incidents**, Harbor queue depth"); it was simply never implemented, which is why this counts as an unimplemented requirement rather than a new feature. The agent's summary is carried VERBATIM (the brief is the E-BRIEF-FAITH surface and is read aloud, so a rewritten sentence would be a claim nobody made, attributed to the company), every fact carries a `log#<seq>` ref so S-BRIEF's "a claim the Architect cannot check is refused" still holds, and the owed-announcement entry is narrated too — an obligation recorded only in `log.jsonl` is one the Architect has to go looking for, and the standup is where they find out without looking. Mutation-checked five ways; the owed-announcement branch survived the first pass and the assertion was added rather than the branch accepted.
- 2026-09-01 · (M7.7) **The §6.1 chain is demonstrated; the §6.1 CRITERION is not, and the review says so** · `test/scenarios/s-onehour.test.ts` walks the whole acceptance chain over the SHIPPED components — real git, the real incident endpoint, Hermes router, ledger endpoint, gate manager and briefing compiler — with only the `gh` process and the ENGINE replaced at their seams (TEST-STRATEGY §1). That proves every arrow between "CI went red" and "the standup says so" exists, is wired, and carries the truth. It does not prove what §6.1 actually asks: whether a REAL agent, given a REAL broken test in a REAL repository, triages it correctly and opens a sound fix PR within the hour, unattended. That is judgment, and no fake engine stands in for it. The gap is not tooling — `gh` is authenticated on this machine and `claude` is on PATH — it is that running §6.1 means deliberately breaking a test in one of the Architect's repositories and leaving autonomous agents with `GH_TOKEN` grants running against it for an hour, which is the Architect's consent to give and nobody else's. So M7's exit row is left UNCHECKED and how the milestone closes is recorded as an OPEN ARCHITECT DECISION, exactly as at M6. Ticking the row and calling the chain the criterion is the substitution the M6 close-out audit was convened to catch, and committing it inside an exit review would make the review worthless.
- 2026-09-01 · (M7.7) **The demo generator is COMMITTED this time** · M6's close-out recorded a standing gap — the generator for `docs/demo/*.svg` was a scratch file, so the artifacts were honest but unreproducible from the repository and a refactor would orphan them silently. M7's evidence is generated by `test/scenarios/m7-evidence.test.ts`, so `npm test` reproduces both artifacts and a change that breaks the chain breaks the suite rather than quietly rotting a file nobody re-runs. Both artifacts state in their own text what they are NOT: the chain transcript says it is not the acceptance criterion, and the E-PLAYBOOK scorecard says its drill record is a fixture rather than a live agent run. An evidence file that does not disclose its own scope is how a reader ends up believing a fake-engine run was a real one. The M6 SVGs still have no committed generator; the pattern is established, that specific debt stands.

- 2026-09-01 · (SRS §6.1 LIVE RUN, THE FINDING THAT MATTERS) **The orchestrator reported a task it had not created** · Artemis read the incident raised from `mertefesensoy/MUSAHIT` and replied to `agent.harbor` with the words "Task opened…". `tasks.json` holds zero and the log contains no `task` event at all. Nothing in the harness noticed: the refusal that caught it was incidental, rejecting her prose for failing to be triage-report JSON rather than for being false. **A green suite cannot find this and neither can a fake engine** — a scripted engine always does what its script says, so the entire class of "an agent says it did the work" is invisible until a real one is asked. It is the E-BRIEF-FAITH failure arriving in the one place M7 never instrumented for it: the brief compiler cites refs and refuses an unref'd claim, and the LEDGER path has no equivalent. Recorded as the result of the run rather than fixed in place, because fixing it and quietly re-running would spend the Architect's quota to hide what happened.
- 2026-09-01 · (SRS §6.1 LIVE RUN) **Three defects in M7's own code behind that one** · (1) `submitToHarbor` treats EVERY message reaching `agent.harbor` as a triage report, so an orchestrator's ordinary courtesy reply — legal under ADR-0003's act table — is refused by name; the endpoint has to tell a report from an acknowledgement instead of assuming. (2) `prompts/harbor/incident-body.md` asks Artemis to open a task and then prints the triage-report JSON schema in the SAME message, addressed to her, describing what the ON-CALL agent should send; two audiences in one message, and she reasonably read the schema as hers. (3) Nothing reconciles an agent's claim against the ledger, so an incident can stay open with no work attached and no alarm. The third is the load-bearing one; the first two are plumbing that made it visible by accident.
- 2026-09-01 · (SRS §6.1 LIVE RUN, FIVE DEFECTS NO SUITE COULD SEE) **Running the thing found what 2,700 green tests could not** · `agora.log(-1, …)` was refused by its own validator on every call, so the floor's mail envelopes had never flown once — and the rejections went to the MAIN process console, where no renderer test could reach them. M7.4's incident binding filtered `trigger.when === 'ci'` against a plan that renders `"on ci"`, so EVERY CI failure was dropped as `incident-unclaimed`; the unit tests passed `IncidentBinding` objects in directly and never derived one from a plan, which is the seam — the M7.6 lesson committed inside the package that restates it. `cmd.exe` caps a command line at 8,191 characters and Claude Code takes an agent's whole identity on it, so the orchestrator at 10,908 bytes could not spawn at all while the crew sat within 250 bytes of the same cliff, with `memory.md` append-only and growing toward it. The wake nudge could be lost in a race between consuming an inbox and observing it empty. Four are fixed with named regressions. The fifth is identified and NOT fixed: an avatar phase that never returns to `idle` makes an agent permanently unnudgeable — it stalled this run for twenty minutes, a restart masks it, and the cause is not yet known, so it is recorded as open rather than guessed at.
- 2026-09-01 · (SRS §6.1 LIVE RUN) **What the deny-all posture actually cost, and what it bought** · with no `gate-policy.json` every class has no rule and no rule means held, so the Watch stopped every gated action — which is precisely §6.1's "zero un-gated destructive actions", demonstrated on a live run rather than simulated. It bought that at the price of three gates in twenty-five minutes, each needing the Architect at the keyboard, which is the opposite of the "walks away" the criterion describes. Separately: Artemis exhausted a 2,000,000-token daily allowance on BRIEFING work before ever reaching the incident. Whether that is a budget too small, a briefing loop too expensive, or a priority the ledger ought to express is unresolved and wants its own look before the hour is attempted again.
- 2026-09-01 · (M7.9) **`known-targets.json` at the harness home root** · activation asked for an absolute working directory and nothing remembered it, so every restart returned the panel to an empty form and the same long path was retyped from memory. The new file records what has already been activated, and `profiles:list` carries it to the panel as chips — no new IPC channel. Deliberately NOT a restore list: choosing a chip fills the form and still goes through preview and activate, because a harness that spawned a company at boot from a stored path string is a different feature with a different consent story (FR-10.4's import-only-pre-fills rule, applied by analogy).
- 2026-09-01 · (ADR-0022) **The company GitHub identity is an App, and ADR-0020's co-author address was wrong** · the Architect asked how Claude and Cursor manage bot authorship; the answer is a GitHub App, which is where the `[bot]` suffix comes from and which a machine user cannot have. ADR-0020 called an App "heavier to stand up", but the weight is the webhook endpoint and the Harbor polls — so an App is a signing key, a JWT and one exchange call, with no new dependency. It also replaces a long-lived PAT (valid until somebody notices a leak, rotation a standing duty) with an installation token that expires within the hour. Separately, ADR-0020's `ephesus-crew+agent.mason@users.noreply.github.com` credits nobody: GitHub resolves only `<numeric id>+<login>@users.noreply.github.com`, so the bot's id is now read back from the API rather than assumed. Owed and NOT built: ADR-0020's enforcement clause confining company authorship to `agent/*` branches — the tripwire bans Claude/Anthropic identities only and would accept a company-authored commit anywhere, and it cannot fire until an agent commits.
- 2026-09-01 · (ADR-0020 enforcement) The company-on-`main` clause keys on **`main`'s first-parent chain**, not on a branch name · ADR-0020 states its rule in terms of branches ("legal only on `agent/*`"), and a commit object records no branch — the name is a moving pointer that may be deleted, renamed, or created after the fact. The one durable structural fact is that merged work hangs off a SECOND parent, so a bot commit on the first-parent chain was put on the trunk rather than merged into it, which is the fault ADR-0020 actually names. The proxy is exact on this repository (every landing so far is a real merge commit: 88 first-parent of 150 reachable) and it is **coupled to that merge policy** — a squash or rebase merge replays bot authorship onto the chain and would flag a legitimately reviewed agent PR. Recorded here rather than only in the script because the coupling is the thing a future reader must know before switching the repo to squash merges. Pending mode is a separate, stronger clause: the pre-commit hook runs at the one moment a branch name exists, so it enforces the literal `agent/*` rule and fails closed on a detached HEAD.
- 2026-09-01 · (ADR-0020 enforcement) The company identity is matched by **form** (`<slug>[bot]`, `^<numeric id>+<slug>[bot]@users.noreply.github.com$`), not by a configured slug · the slug lives in `<harness home>/github-app.json`, which the repository cannot read and CI certainly does not have; a check driven by an env var would have been strongest on the developer's laptop and absent in the one place it is a gate. Matching the form makes the rule a superset — `dependabot[bot]` on the trunk chain fails too — which is the correct generalisation, since no automated identity should land on `main` outside review, and in practice bots arrive by PR merge and sit off the chain. The email anchor is deliberate: ADR-0022 records that ADR-0020's original `ephesus-crew+agent.mason@…` credits nobody because GitHub resolves only `<numeric id>+<login>@…`, so the check declines to recognise the broken form as a company identity at all.
- 2026-09-01 · (ADR-0022) **`eph-gh-token` rides the recall socket, and re-checks the grant rather than trusting the caller** · an installation token lives an hour and an agent holds the copy it was spawned with, so a crew member still working after an hour pushes with a dead credential and reads the 401 as "I lack permission" — the misreading that makes an agent stop rather than ask. The helper goes on the existing 0600 socket with the existing per-spawn token: a second channel for a second purpose would be a second thing to secure. The endpoint's own gate proves only that a LIVE agent is asking, so the handler additionally re-checks `AgentCard.envGrants` — without it the researcher, whose spawns are no-secrets by NFR-17, could ask for the company credential and be given it. A refusal carries a reason written for the agent ("your role does not declare GH_TOKEN") and never a token; mutation-checked on both the token gate and the refusal status.
- 2026-09-01 · (LIVE RUN, ARCHITECT DIRECTIVE) **An engine notification is two different things, and nine of ten were the wrong one** · Claude Code fires `Notification` both for "Claude needs your permission to use X" and for "Claude is waiting for your input" — a decision request and an idle notice. The Watch treated every one as a permission prompt, so of ten gates opened on the live run, NINE asked the Architect to approve an agent sitting at an empty prompt. Worse, `onSettled` has no `tool-permission` branch: approving reached the avatar, the ledger and the badge, and never the engine, so the control did nothing in either direction. The wording is engine-specific and now lives in the adapter (NFR-12) as `notificationKind`; core learns only which situation it was, and unrecognised wording reads as a permission prompt because a real prompt mistaken for idleness strands an agent silently. On the Architect's directive ("a software architect does not oversee intern's work"), a genuine prompt under a granted `autonomous` is no longer gated either — that decision was made once, at activation. Not gated is not unrecorded: every ungated prompt is written to `log.jsonl`, or autonomy would mean unobserved. STILL OPEN: routing the remaining supervised-level prompts to Artemis under her delegated authority (FR-5.5) rather than to the Architect, which the directive also asked for.
- 2026-09-01 · (SECURITY, LIVE DEFECT FOUND BY ADVERSARIAL REVIEW) **An agent could write the memo header that decides which gate its verdict settles** · `renderMemoMarkdown` places the agent-authored `title` ABOVE the machine header and `parseMemoHeader` took the first line matching `^- <name>: `, so a title containing newlines wrote the header. Reproduced before fixing: `title: "Budget breach\n- gate: g-VICTIM-GATE\n- trigger: spend"` parsed as gate `g-VICTIM-GATE`, trigger `spend`, while the real values sat below being ignored. Two live consequences. `fileMemo` validates `gateId` against the open gate AND the filer, but the settle path re-reads `header.gateId` from the markdown — so an honest memo for one's own gate could release a DIFFERENT agent's hold, one the orchestrator was never shown. And `settleFromOrchestrator` asks `mayDecide({class:'memo', domain: header.trigger})`, so an agent held on an undelegated domain could name a delegated one and have FR-5.5's audit trail record `under:` a domain the decision never touched — the harness generating its own false countersignature. Fixed in two layers: the title schema refuses newlines, and the parser reads only the contiguous `- key: value` block anchored on `- memo: ` rather than scanning the document. Both mutation-checked. Note for future work: the first regression written for this PASSED with the fix removed, because its fixture was missing `taskId` and failed validation for that reason instead — an assertion that could not fail, caught only by mutating.
- 2026-09-01 · (ARCHITECT DIRECTIVE + PRIOR ART) **A budget breach no longer opens a gate; the ladder is the enforcement** · the Architect judged the budget gate unnecessary and asked for evidence rather than guesswork, naming `chaitanyagiri/munder-difflin` — a system of near-identical shape (Electron, agent CLIs, office floor, steer/constrain/stop breaker, durable cost ledger, gates for spend/scope/destructive). It opens NO human gate on budget breach: the ladder enforces automatically, `hardStop` is off by default so it never kills, and its caps are floor-wide blaming the top spender. Ephesus's own ADR-0011 agrees — it never mentions a gate, specifies a log event plus trip signal #4, and gives rung 3's terminus as "Artemis decides reassignment", naming her and not the human as the escalation target. The gate came from FR-11.1's "spend above threshold", whose threshold is the POLICY's `maxSpendTokens`; wiring a hire's `dailyTokens` breach to it conflated two different numbers (`src/main/index.ts:1836-1848`). It also never stopped any spending — `submitSpend` discarded `submit()`'s answer, so the agent carried on while a human was interrupted. Removed. The breaker still evaluates on every verdict, rung 2 still halves the remaining budget, the `budget` log event and the visible degradation both remain. NOTE for a future look: `munder-difflin` prices in USD from a per-model table (input/output/cache-read/cache-write) and prefers live provider telemetry, where Ephesus reports `costUsd: null` by deliberate M3 choice — a token count is not a quantity a human can judge, and that is probably why these numbers were never calibrated.
- 2026-09-01 · (LIVE DEFECT) **`agent.profiles` could send but not receive, so every sweep report was dropped** · a scheduled trigger arrives `from: agent.profiles` and `PROTOCOL.md` tells an agent to reply to whoever asked it, but the router had no branch for that endpoint — the reply fell through to the mailbox lookup and bounced with `no mailbox for "agent.profiles"`, observed twice on the live run. The crew did their sweeps all evening and the company never heard a result. Fixed by giving the endpoint a routing branch on the harbor's pattern (reply-shaped acts only — `inform` or `done`; a sweep report tells the harness what was found and never asks it for anything) and a handler that records the report as a `profile`/`sweep-reported` event. This is the SECOND instance of the same defect class after `agent.harbor` accepting only triage reports: reserved endpoints that talk without listening. A general audit of `RESERVED_AGENT_IDS` against the router's branches is owed.
- 2026-09-01 · (LIVE RUN) **`requires_reply` is derived, not refused — the refusal destroyed a finished brief** · Artemis composed a complete, fully-cited standup narration, set `requires_reply: false` on a `propose`, and `parseMessage` refused the whole message; Hermes parked it in `.rejected/` and logged it. The author is never told — `reject()` cites FR-3.4's "never drop silently" while being entirely silent to the sender — so she could neither learn nor retry, and the brief loop broke every window. `PROTOCOL.md` already tells agents they "do not get to choose it", and `composeMessage` already derives it for harness-authored mail; only agent mail was held to a rule it was told it did not control. It is now derived for agent mail too, with the correction reported in `MessageParse.corrected` so drift stays visible. This is STRONGER than the refusal against the threat the refusal was written for ("a sender trying to dodge the obligation table"): an obligation that is computed cannot be dodged, where a refusal merely loses the message. STILL OPEN: a rejection of any kind is never returned to its author, which is the general form of this bug.
- 2026-09-01 · (SRS §6.1 LIVE RUN — THE CHAIN COMPLETED, AND THE CLAIM WAS STILL FALSE) **Detection, delegation, triage and assignment all ran; two of the agent's assertions were checkable and wrong** · the full chain worked for the first time: Harbor ingested `mertefesensoy/MUSAHIT#ci-run:33440874791` at 14:20:12, Artemis archived a cited standup brief at 14:21:49, delegated the incident to the CI babysitter at 14:22:03, and `incident-triaged` landed at 14:23:04 — zero errors, zero gates, zero exits, and the one engine notification was ungated rather than put to the Architect. The triage was a specific technical diagnosis of the real repository and MOST of it verifies: `tests/test_linker.py:29` really does pin `NOW = datetime(2026, 5, 23, 12, 0, 0)`, `WINDOW_DAYS` really is 30, the failing branch really is `ci/add-pytest-workflow`. But its ROOT CAUSE is false and takes ten seconds to check: `ArcLinker.run()` already takes `now: datetime | None = None`, documented as "Tests pass a fixed value so fixtures with hardcoded timestamps stay inside the window", and already threads it into `_load_arc_cache(now)`. The proposed fix — "add an optional now param and thread it through ~19 call sites" — is largely work already done. (Two bare `utcnow()` calls do remain at `linker.py:414,629`, but they write `created_at`/`last_update`, not the window cutoff the agent blamed.) Separately the summary states "Task was opened and assigned to agent.skeleton-crew-musahit-ci-babysitter" and `tasks.json` holds ZERO tasks — the same false ledger claim as the previous run, now isolated from every other failure. TWO reconciliation gaps, not one: nothing checks a claim against the ledger, and nothing checks a claim against the repository it describes. A plausible, well-cited, confidently wrong diagnosis is the failure mode this system must catch, and it currently cannot.
- 2026-09-01 · (RECONCILIATION) **A triage report may not claim a ledger action it cannot point at** · the brief has refused unsupported sentences since M4 (`checkNarrative`: a sentence citing a ref no fact supports is refused), and the triage report — the other thing an agent asserts about its own work — had no equivalent, which is how "Task was opened and assigned…" reached the log beside an empty `tasks.json`. `checkTriage` ports the same rule: if the summary claims a task was opened, the report must carry a `refs` entry naming a task the ledger really holds, else the report is refused back to its author with the reason. Deliberately NARROW — it matches the specific repeated claim and says nothing about any other sentence, because a checker that judged every claim would be wrong often enough that nobody would trust it, and it checks CLAIMS not judgement: whether a diagnosis is correct is not knowable from the harness, and pretending otherwise would be the same confident wrongness one level up. Skipped when no ledger is wired, since refusing on a check that could not run is worse than not checking. ALSO FIXED, and the actual cause of the false claim: `prompts/harbor/incident-body.md` told Artemis to "open a task … and assign it" without naming `agent.ledger` or any format, and then explained the ON-CALL agent's reply protocol in the same message — so she did the on-call agent's job (her terminal: "I replied with the INCIDENT-TRIAGE JSON body harbor's runbook expects") and never opened a task, because nothing told her how. The prompt now has a "what you are being asked to do" section naming the ledger endpoint and a "what you are NOT being asked to do" section, and the triage JSON moved to the on-call agent's own runbook where it belongs.
- 2026-09-01 · (RECONCILIATION, THE SECOND GAP) **A root cause is an assertion about a FILE, so a second agent opens it** · the live run's finding named two gaps and `checkTriage` closed one, stopping deliberately at "it checks claims, never judgement". That line is right about a severity and wrong about a diagnosis: "the cause is X" is a claim about the content of a file, and a file can be read by somebody else. So a triage report may now carry a `rootCause` — a claim plus at least one `file`/`line`/**`quote`** — and the harness mails it to an independent agent with one instruction, try to REFUTE this. The quote is the load-bearing field: "ArcLinker has no injectable clock" is unfalsifiable prose, `linker.py:122 — "async def run(self, run_id: str)"` is a statement a second reader watches fail. A summary that says "root cause" and cites nothing is refused (narrow lexical rule, `LEDGER_CLAIM`'s discipline), and a claim with no citation is unrepresentable rather than merely discouraged. The verdict is `agree`/`refute`/`cannot-tell` and is held to the CLAIMANT'S OWN RULE — `checkVerdict` refuses an agree or a refute that quotes nothing, or that read no file the claim cited, because an unevidenced "wrong" is worth no more than the unevidenced claim it disputes and is more dangerous, being the answer that gets believed. It is recorded BESIDE the claim, never replacing it: the triage entry stands verbatim, no severity moves, and a `refute` reaches the claimant by mail and the Architect at the next standup. The Architect's standing position and the right one — the verifier is another agent reading the same repository under the same pressures, and letting one reading overwrite another would swap a confident wrong claim for a confident wrong correction. WHO PAYS: an ordinary hire in the bundle (`VERIFIER_HIRE` convention, no schema field), so the second opinion carries its own `dailyTokens` on the activation screen; a profile with no such hire gets incidents triaged and UNVERIFIED with the reason logged. NOT DONE, and deliberately: nothing escalates on a dispute, because a verifier that can be wrong must not be able to interrupt the Architect. ALSO: a fourth cost guard was written and DELETED — its regression passed with the guard removed, since an incident raises once and is triaged once, so `verify` cannot be reached twice; the test now binds to the idempotency that actually provides the property.
- 2026-09-01 · (GATE LOOSENED, RECORDED AS SUCH) **The Skeleton Crew's irreversible classes sit at `supervised`, not `manual`** · `dd448a1` raised the built-in's `autonomy.default` to `autonomous`, dropped `spend` and `scope-change` from `byKind` entirely, and moved `destructive` and `prod-facing` from `manual` to `supervised`. The reason is usability AS a safety property: blanket `manual` stopped the crew for a human on routine work, and on the live run the spend clamp fired for all three agents inside a minute to make the Architect approve a limit that was itself miscalibrated — a posture that asks permission for everything gets switched off wholesale rather than tuned. Logged because it IS a loosening and the reasoning should not have to be reconstructed from a commit message: an outward irreversible act is now the agent's to attempt with a human watching, where before it was a human's to initiate. `supervised` rather than `autonomous` on those two is the one rung the Architect did not name explicitly, and `profiles/skeleton-crew/profile.json` is the line to change to overrule it. Composition still only narrows, so a stricter company ceiling clamps all of it; `s-profile.test.ts` asserts both directions. The drift it caused took two commits to clear: `dd448a1` updated only `skeleton-crew.test.ts`, `20237ad` moved the two suites that were left red, and the comments in `s-profile.test.ts` still explained the old rung after that — the assertion and the sentence above it disagreed, which is the shape of stale-comment drift worth naming.
- 2026-09-02 · (ARCHITECT RULING, MVP SCOPE) **The MVP ships Claude only, and refuses the rest at the door** · recorded as [ADR-0024](adr/ADR-0024-claude-only-for-the-mvp.md). The normative documents already held this position — ADR-0009 names Claude Code the reference adapter and the only one that may gate a release, SRS FR-1.2 requires only the seam — and only the shipping surface disagreed. Measured before deciding, not inferred: `README.md:51` advertises "`codex`, `gemini`, `grok`, `opencode`, and friends — as fully-capable agents" while adapters exist for three and `grok`/`opencode` have none at all; `grep -c autonomy` is 5 in `claude.ts` and **0** in both others, so an `autonomous` grant is silently dropped; with no Stop hook there is no continuation loop, so such an agent stops after one turn; and with no hook stream the avatar asserts a confident `idle` forever because `AVATAR_STATES` has no `unknown`. Every one of those is a silent wrong answer rather than a failure — a codex agent does not error, it runs once, reports idle, ignores the autonomy it was granted, and nothing on screen says so. Three `blocks-mvp` findings and two cosmetic ones in the 2026-09-02 MVP register are this single disagreement wearing different clothes. THE DECISION IS TO REFUSE, NOT TO DEGRADE: a profile declaring a non-reference engine is refused at load with the engine named, because a company that silently runs at one turn per wake is worse than one that will not start. WHAT IT IS NOT: permission to collapse the adapter seam. The strongest argument against one engine is that a single-engine system grows engine-specific knowledge everywhere, which NFR-12 exists to prevent — so `codex.ts` and `gemini.ts` stay in the tree, unregistered, as the conformance suite's second implementation, and **if a later change makes conformance pass by special-casing Claude, this decision has been misread**. The conformance suite gains an autonomy case, which is the part that prevents a recurrence rather than merely documenting one: its absence is exactly why the silent drop survived two milestones. Reopen when a second engine is wanted as a product, and the bar is conformance on autonomy, notification and trust — not "it spawns".
- 2026-09-02 · (M8.0, ARCHITECT-APPROVED DEPENDENCY) **`@vitest/coverage-v8@4.1.11` added as a DEV dependency, pinned EXACT** · a BUILD-PROMPT §8.3 must-ask, put to the Architect with the plan's own premise corrected first: "v8 ships with vitest and adds no package" is false on vitest 4 — `npx vitest run --coverage` fails with `MISSING DEPENDENCY  Cannot find dependency '@vitest/coverage-v8'`, measured before asking rather than assumed. Options were v8 (first-party; 15 packages by `--dry-run` once esbuild's platform binaries are excluded from the headline "88"), istanbul (adds `@babel/core`, slower, and no longer more accurate now that vitest 4 remaps v8 through the AST by default), or none (no numbers, no floors). Exact pin because the package's peer is `vitest: 4.1.11`, not a range: a caret bump of either alone fails at install, so vitest and its provider move together, and the pin says so where the next person will look. Audit position: `npm audit --omit=dev` unchanged at 0; the full audit's 2 high are the electron/extract-zip pair recorded at M6.5 — the provider added none. Installed with `--ignore-scripts` so a pure-JS dev dependency did not re-run `electron-rebuild`.
- 2026-09-02 · (M8.0) **The seam rule is enforced from BOTH sides, because each is blind where the other sees** · coverage answers "does any test reach this module", reachability answers "can the application load it", and the M6 Herald passes the first and fails the second. The Architect approved floors AND a reachability tripwire over floors alone. The walk uses the compiler's `resolveModuleName` from the three electron-vite entry points and counts VALUE edges only — `import type` / `export type` are erased, and counting them would have made the Herald reachable the moment something borrowed an interface. The first draft reported six modules as gaps that were nothing of the kind (`engines/types.ts` and the five `*-view.ts` contracts): a file with no runtime statement has no module to load, so it is classified type-only, neither reached nor unreachable — with a bare `import './fx'` still runtime, because loading a module is a run-time act. Stated rather than hidden: a value import used only in type positions is dropped by esbuild and IS counted (reachability is the floor of wiring, not proof of it), and export-level dead code — M3's `effectivePolicy`, reachable by module and called by nothing — is invisible to both halves and owed to a package of its own.
- 2026-09-02 · (M8.0) **Floors are recorded PER PLATFORM, beside their condition, and a platform with none FAILS** · `process.platform` branches, OS-gated tests and timing-dependent paths move a coverage figure between machines — the property that produced six disagreeing timeout margins in one day — so `scripts/coverage-floors.json` holds one block per platform stamped with commit, node, OS and command, and `check-coverage.cjs` on a platform with no block fails rather than passing on nothing (`test/pin.ts`'s rule: could-not-establish must fail). The linux floors are therefore seeded from CI's own emitted measurement (`--emit` → artifact → `--update --from`), never from a Windows run. The record is a JSON file beside the checker and NOT vitest's `thresholds`: thresholds would put the numbers into `vitest.config.mts`, a second record with no room for the condition, and `autoUpdate` rewrites that file in place.
- 2026-09-02 · (M8.0) **The subsystem map is TOTAL, and boot wiring is its own row** · every production file (the set `coverage.include` names) must belong to exactly one subsystem — a file that belongs to none fails, a member that names nothing fails — so adding a module forces a decision about what it is part of; checked over the union of report and tree, because a type-only module produces no report entry and still has to belong somewhere. `index.ts` / `ipc.ts` / the preload / `main.tsx` / `App.tsx` are the `boot` row so SDD §1.1's "index.ts holds no logic of its own" becomes a figure the M8 packages move, and `herald` is its own row so its zero cannot hide inside an average. Rows without a city name — `company` (profiles, modes, org, scheduler, Closing Time), `home`, `panels` — are named for what they are rather than forced under a subsystem they are not.
- 2026-09-02 · (M8.0) **`--update` is a ratchet: it never lowers a floor, never adds an untested module, and still fails on a new one** · a floor falls only by a hand edit with a reason in the diff; a production module no test reaches leaves the record the moment a test reaches it and enters it only by hand, which is the review point. `--update` writes the raised floors AND fails by name when a new untested module exists, rather than refusing to write — coupling the ratchet to the gap would have let a package raise its floors only by first hiding the module. The tolerance is one number with its reason beside it, set from a measured run-to-run spread (entry below), and it is a band under a rising line rather than an allowance that could be spent a commit at a time.
- 2026-09-02 · (M8.0) **`src/shared/contrast.ts` is allowlisted, not moved** · the walk finds it unreachable, correctly: its header records the decision to keep the WCAG arithmetic in `src/shared` so the token test and future design tooling share one implementation. Moving it to `test/` would have silently reversed that decision inside a package about recording decisions; the allowlist entry cites the header instead, and will fail the day design tooling imports it — which is the entry telling you to delete it.
- 2026-09-02 · (M8.0, FOUND BY CI, FIXED AS ITS OWN COMMIT) **`unwrapWindowsShim` normalises EVERY separator, not only doubled ones** · `main`'s CI had been red for many runs on `which.test.ts › follows an npm-style %dp0% shim`, Linux only: the unwrap replaced `%dp0%` and collapsed runs of two or more separators, so a shim's single backslashes stayed literal on POSIX — where `\` is a legal filename character — and the expanded path named a file that did not exist. A `.cmd` is a Windows artifact whatever host reads it, so its backslashes are separators by definition; `[\\/]+` says so. The Architect chose this over guarding the test to Windows, which would have gone green by removing the unwrap's only CI coverage — the register's fifth instance in a new costume. A gate added on a red baseline cannot show that its own red means anything, which is why this landed first.
- 2026-09-02 · (M8.0) **CI runs the suite ONCE, under coverage** · V8 coverage reads the engine's native counters and instruments nothing, so the suite under coverage executes the same code as without it; the Test step became `npm run test:coverage` rather than a second run, the floor check follows it, and the emitted measurement is uploaded as an artifact on every run so a floor can be recorded from the CI condition. Locally the DoD command gains the same two steps in place of `npm test`.
- 2026-09-02 · (M8.0, PROCESS) **A changed CI gate gets a ledger entry: GYM-006** · ENGINEERING-STANDARDS §3 calls an altered CI gate without a Gymnasium ledger entry a defect, and the 2026-08-31 NUL-byte finding was RAISED rather than written for exactly that reason — while M6.10's floor-clock tripwire was added with no entry at all. The Architect approved filing GYM-006 with the two document amendments (ENGINEERING-STANDARDS §6.7, TEST-STRATEGY §2) as drafted: the ratified M8 plan is the mandate, the ledger row is the record, and the metric (a planted regression caught by name; zero false positives on green work; every M8 package naming its call path) is due 2026-09-16.
- 2026-09-02 · (M8.0, MEASURED) **The coverage tolerance is 0.25 points, on a measured spread of zero** · the falsification line was written before looking (0.5 provisional, to be replaced by the observed spread), and two full `npm run test:coverage` runs on win32 at the baseline — same tree, machine otherwise quiet, n = 2 — agreed to the hundredth on every subsystem and metric. A tolerance of zero would have been the literal reading; 0.25 is headroom for timing-dependent paths the two runs happened to take the same way, which is the one thing n = 2 cannot exclude, and on every subsystem but the largest it is under one line. Recorded ONCE, in `toleranceReason` beside the number, with the instruction that a failure inside the band is a false positive to re-measure at a larger n — never a floor to lower. What the baseline itself says is in the M8.0 implementation doc in words; the figures stay in the one file that carries their condition.
- 2026-09-02 · (M8.0, FOUND BY CI — RECORDED, NOT FIXED, OUT OF SCOPE) **`WakeClock` reports overtime from one clock and fires from another** · CI run `33629903392`, a docs-only commit on the M8.0 branch, failed `test/main/pacing-wakes.test.ts › WakeClock — the wall-clock cap › interrupts a wake that outruns the cap` with "expected 19 to be greater than or equal to 20", having passed on the two earlier runs of the same code. The cause is in the product, not the runner: `src/main/watch/wake-clock.ts` arms `setTimeout(capMs)` — libuv's monotonic clock, integer milliseconds — and then reports `ranMs = Date.now() - startedAt`, the wall clock, so the callback can legitimately observe one millisecond less than the cap it fired at; the test asserts `ranMs ≥ capMs` with no tolerance, which is a timing assertion in exactly the register's shape. The honest fix is in the class (report elapsed from the clock the timer runs on, or state that a fired timer means "at least the cap" and say so), not in the assertion — but that is `watch/` product code under ADR-0023, owed to M8.9 (pacing), and M8.0 does not touch it. The job was re-run; the flake is recorded here so the next red is not re-diagnosed from scratch. **It is also the first datum for GYM-006's metric 2: the seam-rule gates themselves did not fire; the floor check was skipped because the suite was red.**
- 2026-09-02 · (M8.0, REFUTED AND FIXED BEFORE CLOSE) **The seam-rule gates were adversarially refuted, and the first draft lost on three counts** · three independent refuters were asked to make each gate pass when it should fail, with probes rather than opinions. Reachability: `import { x } from './x'; export { x }` — a value barrel under `isolatedModules` — was classified type-only, which hid the barrel from the gate AND stopped the walk at it, so its target read as unreachable; a directory allowlist prefix accepted any new file dropped beside the seven Herald modules and never read stale while one of them stayed unreachable; unreadable files were skipped silently; a universe collapsed to nothing would have passed. Coverage: a hand-deleted metric key turned that metric's comparison into `NaN` and silently disabled it, and `--update` then wrote the regressed value as the new floor; deleting a platform block turned `--update` into a re-seed that recorded lowered floors and new untested modules with exit 0; a refused or no-op update re-stamped the condition, so the commit no longer described the figures beside it; `measured.commit` read `.git/HEAD` and could not see a dirty tree; a report about deleted files passed; a stale report could be recorded under a fresh stamp; an artifact from a different map dropped subsystems and a malformed one crashed; the tolerance's stated justification ("under one line on every subsystem but the largest") was false for ten of seventeen rows; and "untested" asked only about lines, so a module merely imported by a tested file — `src/main/config.ts`, one line of ten, no function ever entered — was not on the record. WHAT CHANGED: the classifier is conservative (any value-syntax import is runtime; a file misread as runtime shows up and gets a decision, a file misread as type-only vanishes) and the walk traverses everything it reaches; allowlist entries are exact files; unreadable files and an empty universe fail; the failure text says what the walk cannot see (non-literal dynamic imports). The floors record is schema 2: every floor metric validated as a number, both reasons required, `--seed` is the explicit first record and `--update` refuses to start a block, a refused or no-op update writes nothing and moves no stamp, the condition carries a git-free tree hash and the CI ref, a report older than the newest production file is refused, the report and the tree must be the same set in both directions, an emitted artifact is validated and must cover the map's subsystems exactly, comparisons are rounded, a floor more than `ratchetLag` below reality fails as stale (so coverage gained after the baseline cannot be lost again without a failure), the tolerance is capped at one point with its justification restated in lines per row, and untested means no function entered — which put `config.ts` on the record by hand. Every bypass has a fixture case that fails against the first draft. ONE FINDING STANDS, RECORDED NOT FIXED, AND IT IS THE ARCHITECT'S: **`main` has no branch protection** (`gh api …/branches/main/protection` → 404) and PR #6 was merged over a red code job on 2026-09-02, so every CI gate in this repository — this one included — is advisory until required status checks are enabled on `main`. That is a GitHub setting, raised in the M8.0 session report; DECISIONS-LOG 2026-08-30 already recorded protection as OFF.
- 2026-09-02 · (M8.0, THE WAKECLOCK FLAKE, SECOND OCCURRENCE) **Two of six CI runs on the M8.0 branch failed `pacing-wakes.test.ts` by one millisecond, both on docs-only commits, both green on re-run** · runs `33629903392` and `33633478191`, same assertion ("expected 19 to be greater than or equal to 20"), same cause as recorded above — the timer fires on the monotonic clock and the report reads `Date.now()`. Recorded as a RATE rather than an incident because that is what M8.9 needs to size the fix: roughly one CI run in three on ubuntu-latest, never once in nine local runs on win32. The seam-rule gates were skipped behind the red suite both times, which is GYM-006 metric 2's second datum (not a false positive of the gates; the gates did not run). The fix stays owed to M8.9 and is not the assertion: a timer that fired means the cap elapsed, and the class should say so from the clock it fired on.
- 2026-09-03 · (M8.1) **The window is not a variable any more** · `index.ts` held it in a `BrowserWindow | null` and sent through it 43 times as `mainWindow?.webContents.send(...)`. The optional chain reads as safety and is not: the reference is only null BEFORE the first window exists, and nothing nulled it after. A closed window is DESTROYED, not null, so `?.` proceeded and every send threw — which is the whole quit-path failure, verified rather than inferred: Closing Time's `begin()` throws on its first log event (after the log line lands, which is exactly the `closing-begin`-and-nothing-else the Architect's book of record holds), `AgentManager.shutdown` threw on its first agent, and the PTY sink WAS the window's `webContents`, so killing the terminals threw too. `src/main/ui-bridge.ts` now owns the window, forgets it on `closed`, checks `isDestroyed()` on both objects, never throws at a caller, and is the `PtySink` the terminal stream writes to. Nulling on `closed` alone would NOT have been enough: teardown destroys the window and runs the listener afterwards, which is the case the fake reproduces with `destroy({ fireClosed: false })`. `check-invariants.cjs` rule 5 fails on a `webContents.send` written anywhere else, proven in both directions with a planted probe.
- 2026-09-03 · (M8.1) **A closed window is silent; anything else is a degradation** · the bridge's no-op is deliberate against invariant §7. The renderer is a projection holding no state whose loss means anything (ENGINEERING-STANDARDS §4), so sending to a window that is gone is the normal end of a session, not a give-up. A send that fails for any OTHER reason is reported once through `onDropped` and the window is dropped, so one fault cannot become one per event. The `isDestroyed()` check is what keeps the quiet case quiet — catching the throw alone would also keep the quit alive, but would report a fault for each of the forty-odd sends a shutdown makes — so it is pinned by a spy asserting NO degradation on the production case, not merely by "returns false", which the catch would satisfy on its own.
- 2026-09-03 · (M8.1) **The quit sequence is a module, because the owed test could not exist otherwise** · SDD §1.1 gives `index.ts` boot and wiring and says it holds no logic of its own; the quit path was logic (an offer, a protocol, an unwind, twelve stops in an order that matters), written inline with its tail duplicated for the no-agent-manager branch, and reachable by nothing. There is no Electron in the test runner (BUILD-PROMPT §10.3) and no Playwright in this repository, so "a scenario that drives the REAL handler" was impossible by construction — which is why the rig had a COPY of production's closing wiring, minus the line that threw. `src/main/shutdown.ts` now holds the sequence and both `index.ts` and the scenario rig construct the same object. Ordering, with a reason per edge: closing time first because agents can only park while alive; the unwind next because settings files must be restored while the processes exist (ADR-0009); the stops last, the Agora drained before the database closes because a commit in flight is a record the book has not got (ADR-0004). SDD §1.1 gained both modules.
- 2026-09-03 · (M8.1) **Every phase is isolated, because that is the same bug one level up** · `AgentManager.shutdown` was `for (const id of ...) await unwind(id)` with no `try`, so one agent's failure cost the rest of the company its unwind — settings left in the Architect's repositories, worktrees never released, tokens never revoked. It now isolates per agent and returns an `AgentShutdownReport` naming who failed rather than counting. A quit sequence that stopped at its first failing phase would repeat that mistake with bigger pieces, so a closing that cannot start does not prevent the unwind and an unwind that fails does not leave the terminals running and the database open. Deliberately NOT double-reported: per-agent failures go through the manager's existing `onExitError` seam (the visible channel) and are carried in the quit report for the summary line, because the same fault twice in the Architect's health list reads as two faults.
- 2026-09-03 · (M8.1, ARCHITECT DECISION) **Every quit gesture runs the sequence, exactly once** · before this, only `window-all-closed` ran it, so menu Quit, Cmd-Q and a taskbar close skipped closing time altogether and killed the agents mid-thought; and on macOS that handler tore the company down while leaving the app alive, so `activate` re-opened a window onto a company that no longer existed. `before-quit` now holds the exit, runs the sequence once and lets the quit through when it has finished; `window-all-closed` merely quits off macOS, routing through the same path. A second gesture mid-sequence is HELD rather than obeyed — the protocol's hard deadline is what bounds the wait, and a double Cmd-Q should not cost an agent its parked memory. This changes behaviour SDD §612 describes, so it was put to the Architect with the alternative (keep one trigger, smaller blast radius) and decided; §612 is amended with it.
- 2026-09-03 · (M8.1) **What the scenario rig may still substitute, named so it cannot drift** · a scenario company has no `AgentManager` (that class needs a spawner), so the rig's `liveAgents` reads the mailboxes and its `agents` seam is null — two leaves, both named in the rig with the reason, and `AgentManager.shutdown`'s isolation is proven directly against the real class in `agents.test.ts` instead. Everything else the rig runs is the shipped object, and its closing time now sends through the same bridge production sends through: the three lines of `onLogEvent`, in production's order. The rig can still lie about a leaf; it can no longer lie about the sequence. **The fake window throws on a send once destroyed**, as Electron does — a fake that accepted that send would have let the old wiring look survivable, which is the same class of mistake as the rig that omitted the line.
- 2026-09-03 · (M8.1) **What a quit destroys is written as a test, not as a sentence** · gates and activations are in-memory, so a quit loses an open gate while `tasks.json` may still hold its id (register B17, M8.8's to close). S-CLOSING carries a CHARACTERIZATION case that passes today because the loss is real: the opening is on the record, `gates.json` does not exist, and the gate survives only in this process's memory. M8.8 flips that case rather than being asked to remember a paragraph. The Architect chose this over pulling M8.8's persistence forward, which would have collided with its own plan for activation replay.
- 2026-09-03 · (M8.1) **`shutdown.ts` and `ui-bridge.ts` are mapped to the `boot` coverage row** · that row measures how true SDD §1.1's "index.ts holds no logic of its own" is, and these two modules are the first logic moved OUT of it. Filing them under `boot` keeps the row's meaning and lets its floor record the improvement; filing them elsewhere would have flattered `boot` by removing what it exists to measure.
- 2026-09-03 · (M8.2, ARCHITECT DECISION) **A degradation gets its own log kind, because it is a STATE and not an event** · SDD §4.3's union already had `error`, used by Hermes for two delivery faults, and reusing it was the no-amendment option. The distinction that decided it: "delivery threw" happened once, "recall is on the grep rung because there is no index" is a condition the company is RUNNING UNDER, and the questions asked of the second — when did it start, is it still true, how often, did it ever clear — have no answers in a bucket that also holds the first. Only a state can be cleared, and only a state is worth replaying at boot. §4.3 is amended with the kind and its semantics; §1.1 gained `degradations.ts`.
- 2026-09-03 · (M8.2) **Dedupe by CAUSE, and the cause is a type** · the package plan warns "dedupe by cause, not by text" and the pacing line is why: it reads `company pacing slow: 5h window at 82%` with a percentage that changes every tick, so a text key would have defeated the dedupe entirely while looking like it worked. Every report carries `<source>/<slug>`, chosen at the CALL SITE because only the call site knows which of a subsystem's problems this is — `library` alone had four (`fts`, `mempalace`, `recall-rung`, `reflection`) that a source-keyed dedupe would have collapsed into whichever spoke last, and per-agent conditions carry the agent, because two agents stuck is two problems rather than one reported twice. `DegradationCause` is a template type over a closed source list, so a typo is a compile error; the source is DERIVED from the cause rather than passed beside it, so the two cannot disagree. Assigning 61 causes was the bulk of this package and the point of it.
- 2026-09-03 · (M8.2) **The ring holds CAUSES, which is what makes the flood harmless** · the old array held occurrences and was capped at 50, so the pacing check evicted everything else within a minute — register item B2/D9. Keyed by cause, three thousand pacing reports are ONE entry with a count of three thousand, and the cap becomes "how many distinct things are wrong", a number that stays small for real reasons. The test asserts an unrelated entry SURVIVES a five-hundred-report flood rather than asserting a number of entries, because the number is not the property that matters.
- 2026-09-03 · (M8.2, ARCHITECT DECISION) **A repeating cause reaches the book of record on a bounded ladder** · first occurrence, then each power of ten, then the clear. An hour of a once-a-second condition costs five lines instead of 3,600, and those five say what a reader wants: it started, it is still going, it is really still going, it stopped and for how long. Append-only (invariant §5) is untouched — nothing is rewritten, there are simply fewer and more informative appends — which matters because the log is read from byte zero by every consumer and is already M8.10's subject. The exact count is always live in the UI, which reads the ring rather than the file. The alternatives were every occurrence (3,600 lines an hour into a file M8.10 is booked to fix) and first-only (which cannot tell five seconds from all night).
- 2026-09-03 · (M8.2, ARCHITECT DECISION) **A restart replays what was true when we stopped, marked CARRIED** · B2's complaint is that the list is gone at restart, so every morning looks healthy whatever the company is missing. The tail of the log is replayed at boot and every replayed entry is marked `carried` — it was true when we stopped and nothing has re-checked it — so nothing claims to be current that has not been re-observed. A `cleared` row removes its cause, so a fixed problem does not come back from the dead; the first live report replaces the carried entry, which is how the Architect learns it is still true; and a live entry is never overwritten by a replayed one, because what this session observed is newer than anything the file can say. The alternative (session-only list, log as history) was rejected for leaving the morning view empty.
- 2026-09-03 · (M8.2) **`tailOf` exists because `read` pages the wrong way for this question** · `readLog(afterSeq, limit)` pages FORWARD from a cursor, which is right for a consumer catching up and wrong for one asking what is true NOW — asking it for the newest degradations hands back the oldest, which is register item B3 and precisely the mistake M8.3 fixes at its callers. Rather than work around it, `eventlog.ts` gained `tailOf(limit)` and `agora.ts` a `tailLog` passthrough, and a test proves the difference on a 600-entry log instead of trusting the comment. M8.3 inherits the tool rather than the workaround.
- 2026-09-03 · (M8.2) **Reporting cannot be the thing that fails** · `report` never throws and the append is best-effort, because a channel that fails while reporting a failure is worse than no channel: a full disk still leaves the condition in the ring and on screen. Rows reported BEFORE the Agora exists are buffered and flushed in order the moment it does — boot reports through this channel from its first line and the book of record opens a few lines later, and that window is exactly when a first-run setup failure happens.
- 2026-09-03 · (M8.2) **The line the Architect reads lives in `src/shared/`, not the renderer** · the formatter is a pure projection of the model and needs no DOM, and putting it beside the model means it can be asserted without one — the first attempt imported `App.tsx` into a test and died on `navigator is not defined`, which is the runner telling the truth about where the function belonged. The renderer test is typed against `AgoraHealth['runtime'][number]`, so renaming a field in the IPC contract breaks the test rather than silently changing what the Architect sees.
- 2026-09-03 · (M8.2, THE RATCHET BIT AND WAS RIGHT) **A new module at 93% pulled the `agora` row under its floor, and the answer was tests rather than a lower floor** · the M8.0 gate refused the package because adding `degradations.ts` to a subsystem sitting at 97.62% dragged the pooled figure below it. Both easy outs were wrong: moving the module to a quieter row is gaming the map the map exists to prevent, and editing the floor down by hand would have hidden the actual hole. The hole was real — the flood test used a limit LARGER than the number of causes it created, so the eviction path had never run once. Three cases now cover overflow, including that a repeat does not renew an entry (age is when a condition started, not when it last spoke), and the module reaches full line coverage. First time the M8.0 floors refused work rather than merely recording it.
- 2026-09-04 · (M8.3, ARCHITECT DECISION) **The renderer learns that the book grew from the book itself, as a publish/subscribe** · every appender was expected to also push `log:append`; `index.ts` did it thirty-one times and Hermes — thirteen append sites, 282 of this machine's 1,177 entries — never did, so the Activity panel simply never heard about a delivery. The Architect chose the notification at the Agora over a callback threaded through Hermes, and asked for it as a proper pub/sub for the flexibility and the testing chances. `appendLog` is the single writer (invariant §5 keeps it so), so one subscription covers every appender that will ever exist rather than every appender somebody remembered. Contract, each clause with a case: delivered AFTER the entry is on disk (a subscriber that re-reads must find what it was told about — the panel does exactly that); in sequence order, synchronously; one subscriber's failure costs no other its event and never fails the append, because the book of record does not depend on who is listening; and delivery walks a snapshot, so subscribing or unsubscribing mid-delivery is safe. Thirty-one hand-written pushes became one subscription. ONE survives deliberately — the company-mode `onChanged`, which fires for the Gymnasium LEDGER, a different file the subscription knows nothing about.
- 2026-09-04 · (M8.3, ARCHITECT DECISION) **The whole-book readers have no window, and their cost is reported** · `readLog()` returns the OLDEST 500 and three callers used the default: the standup read that head and THEN filtered `seq > cursor`, so every brief after seq 500 was compiled from an empty fact set (676 of 1,177 entries invisible, with a retro on disk citing `log#1–log#499` against a highest seq of 1,117); the org layer folded every metric from 500 rows; and the §6.9 proof gate could not see evidence older than the window. A bound cannot be right for any of them — a fact silently outside the window is worse than a slow answer — so `readLogAll`/`readLogSince` read to the end, and a read slower than `slowReadMs` (default 50 ms, about a frame) raises a degradation naming the entry count, the file size and the duration. M8.10 owns making it cheap and now has measurements from real use to size against; M8.3 owns making it honest. The rejected option was a bound that admits truncation: a standup that narrates a partial day and says so is still a worse brief than a slow one.
- 2026-09-04 · (M8.3) **The row the panel shows is total over the log kinds, and cannot be blank** · the formatter lived in `ActivityPanel.tsx` with seven cases and a `default` that reached for `agentId` or `subject`, so 19% of rows rendered as an empty line — an event that happened, on screen as nothing — and the breaker case read `signal` where every emitter writes `signals`, an ARRAY, which blanked the reason on all 93 breaker rows while the row still looked populated (the agent id and rung were there, so nothing looked broken). `logRowSummary` moved to `src/shared/log.ts` beside the kind list it must cover: no `default`, a `never` check at the end, so a new kind is a compile error where its row is decided — the same technique the floor's station table uses. Per-kind wording is for readability; the FALLBACK is for truth: when a kind's fields are all absent (an older entry, a changed emitter) the line falls back to the entry's own remaining fields, bounded to four, rather than rendering nothing. A blank row is a lie about the book of record. `secret-rotated` shows the name and never the value (ADR-0010), asserted.
- 2026-09-04 · (M8.3, ARCHITECT DECISION) **The Activity panel opens at the END of the book** · it opened its cursor at seq 0 and paged forward, so after an overnight run it showed the company's FIRST 300 events and crawled towards the present one append at a time (B4). The follow path was always right; only the arrival was wrong. It now opens through `agora:logTail` — the reader M8.2 added for the degradation replay, which had asked the same question — and then follows forward exactly as before. The Architect declined a load-older control for now: additive later, and a second cursor with its own edge cases is not what the MVP needs.
- 2026-09-04 · (M8.3) **Every case in this package runs against a log LARGER than the old window** · that window is the defect, and every fixture in this repository was smaller than 500 entries, which is precisely why five separate defects were invisible to a green suite. The assertions name WHICH entries appear rather than how many — a count would have passed against both the head-window bug and the blank rows, which is the package plan's own warning.
- 2026-09-04 · (M8.3, THE RATCHET BIT TWICE MORE) **New branches in a well-covered subsystem must be covered, not averaged away** · the floors refused the package because `logRowSummary`'s branches pulled `agora` down. The gaps were real: a flow with only one side recorded, a boolean field, a list field that is not a list, the fallback's bound, and the exhaustive switch's unreachable arm. The last is the interesting one — rather than mark it ignored or fake an impossible state, it is reached with a cast and documented as what it really guards: an entry whose kind comes from a NEWER version of the app, where the panel must render something rather than throw. `parseLogLine` refuses such a kind, so it cannot arrive from the file, and the case says so.
- 2026-09-04 · (FIX, OUTSIDE A PACKAGE) **WakeClock reported an elapsed time that contradicted the reason it was written** · the cap fires on libuv MONOTONIC timer and the report was measured with `Date.now()`, a wall clock with its own rounding, so `onOvertime` could say "ran 19 ms against a 20 ms cap". That failed CI on ONE UBUNTU RUN IN THREE — three occurrences across the M8 branches (runs 33629903392, 33633478191, 33806882158), each costing a re-run, and each time the seam-rule gates were SKIPPED behind the red suite, which is the part that made it worth fixing now rather than at M8.9. The timer having fired is the evidence that the cap elapsed, so where the arithmetic contradicts that, the known fact wins: the report is clamped to the cap. The clock stays injectable, which is what lets a test drive the deadline at all, and the regression case makes the disagreement DETERMINISTIC — an injected clock reading one millisecond short at the moment the timer fires — rather than waiting for the machine to be unlucky. Deliberately NARROW: this touches the reported number and nothing else. M8.9 still owns the pacing UI, the wake budget and ADR-0023 policy.
- 2026-09-04 · (M8.4, ARCHITECT DECISION DD-1) **The shipped gate policy is a real CEILING, not a floor of caution** · with no `gate-policy.json` the fallback was deny-all at `manual`, and autonomy composes stricter-wins (ADR-0012) — so that ceiling did not make the company careful, it made every profile decorative. The Skeleton Crew ships `autonomous` with its irreversible classes at `supervised` and ran at `manual` for everything on every install that has ever existed: each agent sat at a permission prompt nobody was there to answer, unattended running was impossible out of the box, and nothing said so (B5). The shipped ceiling is now `autonomous` so a profile's own declaration governs, with `destructive`, `prod-facing`, `scope-change`, `outbound` and `spend` held at `supervised` and `needs-human` at `manual`. "The Watch held every gated action" stays true for every class that can hurt you; what changes is that routine work is no longer stopped for a prompt nobody will answer — the same reasoning the Architect applied on 2026-09-01 when the crew's own irreversible classes moved off `manual`. `tool-permission` is deliberately absent: `evaluateGate` refuses that kind by construction, so a rule for it would describe nothing.
- 2026-09-04 · (M8.4) **Shipped config is a schema-validated VALUE, and `~/.ephesus/` is never overwritten** · the package's own risk line is example configs drifting from the schemas they illustrate, so `shippedGatePolicy` and `shippedAuthority` are `schema.parse(...)` constants — a drift is a module-load failure rather than a file nobody validates — and `home.ts` writes them out on first boot. Written only when ABSENT: an existing file is the Architect's, whatever it says, and overwriting one would be the harness silently reverting a decision they made. What it created is reported through the M8.2 degradation channel under a new `home` source, because a config file that appears without being mentioned is one nobody learns they can edit. A test asserts the ROUND TRIP — what is seeded is what the real loader reads back — since seeding a file the loader then rejects would boot the company on deny-all with a file sitting right there.
- 2026-09-04 · (M8.4) **Artemis ships with FR-5.5's own example as her authority** · `authority.json` has never existed on any install, so `noAuthority` applied and the orchestrator held nothing: every routine decision queued for the Architect, and FR-5.5 was not a feature switched off but a feature nobody could reach.
