The shipped table grants `route` and `task` on every domain — that IS the orchestrator's job (FR-5.1/5.2), and withholding them leaves nobody to do it — plus `memo` on `test-code` and `docs`, which is the requirement's worked example. `gate` is NOT granted, because a gate is the Watch's question to a human and an orchestrator who could answer it would be answering on the Architect's behalf about exactly the classes the Watch holds for them. `spend` is NOT granted: FR-5.5 names it as the example of what she may not have, and it is the one class here that costs money. Everything she does decide is countersigned and archived, so widening the table later never costs the audit trail.
- 2026-09-04 · (M8.4) **The activation preview asks the SAME resolver the spawn asks** · `declaredEnvGrants` returned what a profile declares and stopped there, so the screen promised `GH_TOKEN` on an install with no `github-app.json` and no such secret — an affirmative promise about something that would simply be missing at spawn (B8). The plan now carries `grantsUnavailable`, answered by `resolveDeclaredGrants`, the one function the AgentManager already used: the screen and the outcome cannot disagree because there is nothing for them to disagree about. The parameter is REQUIRED rather than optional — a default of "assume they are all available" is precisely the silent assertion it exists to remove — which cost a fourth argument at every call site and is worth it.
- 2026-09-04 · (M8.4, ARCHITECT DECISION) **A logged-out engine gets its own lifecycle, and is not started** · nothing probed authentication, so a logged-out CLI spawned, printed its own login prompt, sat there all day and reported `running` with a confidently idle avatar (B9). The Architect chose a visible `needs-login` state over refusing the spawn outright or reporting a degradation only: it reuses FR-1.6's existing shape for "the engine is not ready", and the card carries the command that fixes it. The engine is NOT started, so nothing can sit at a prompt while its card claims to work, and the dock says why rather than "no signal yet" — a process that never started has no phase to report, and "no signal yet" reads as "any moment now". Costs a value in `AgentCard.lifecycle` (SDD §3) and a `fixCommand` field.
- 2026-09-04 · (M8.4) **The auth probe is THREE-VALUED and reads the denial first** · yes / no / cannot-tell, and cannot-tell is TRUSTED rather than refused — reading "I could not tell" as "logged out" would refuse to start a healthy company the first time an engine rephrased its status line, which is `test/pin.ts`'s rule applied in the other direction. The manager honours it for a probe that is absent, that throws, or that answers null. And the predicate reads the DENIAL first with positives that demand `logged in as` rather than `logged in`: **`Not logged in` contains `logged in`**, so a bare substring test sends a company to work with no session — the same trap that made `reproduce` match `prod` (M7.4) and made a spoken refusal confirm a gate (M6). Both halves are load-bearing and both are tested, which the mutation pass forced: the substring mutation SURVIVED its first run because the ordering alone caught it, so the case that makes the pattern load-bearing was added and it then died.
- 2026-09-04 · (DEFECT CLEARANCE, ARCHITECT DECISION) **An engine adapter may not match on output this repository has never seen** · M8.4's auth probe was written against imagined output. The installed CLI (`@anthropic-ai/claude-code` 2.1.252) answers `claude auth status` with JSON by DEFAULT — `{"loggedIn": true, …}`, exit 0, and `--help` states `--json` is the default with `--text` opt-in — while `--text` prints `Login method: … / Email: …`. The shipped patterns (`logged in as`, `authenticated as`, `account:`) matched NEITHER, so every real invocation fell through to "cannot tell", `needs-login` could not fire on any machine that has ever existed, and the README told the Architect it was being asked at every spawn. Forty-five tests passed, every one of them fed a string we had written ourselves. Third instance of the shape (`reproduce` matching `prod`, M7.4; a spoken refusal confirming a gate, M6), so the Architect made it a rule rather than a lesson: ENGINEERING-STANDARDS §6.8, enforced by `check-invariants.cjs` rule 6 — every declared probe is backed by a capture in `test/fixtures/engine-output/` with its command, engine version, platform and date in `PROVENANCE.json`, or waived there in writing. Codex and Gemini are the waivers, and the waiver list IS the visible debt. Rejected: scoping the rule to "adapters that ship", which is a moving target decided elsewhere and would stop applying silently the day one is promoted.
- 2026-09-04 · (DEFECT CLEARANCE) **The matcher reads the machine answer first, prose second, and the fixture is a capture rather than a re-serialisation** · JSON `loggedIn` when it is a BOOLEAN — a string `"false"` is not an answer this code will invent one from — then denials before positives (they are substrings of each other), then `null`. Asking for `--text` explicitly was rejected: it breaks on any version predating the flag, which is the same inert feature in a new costume. The fixture is the real stdout with identifying values replaced by SUBSTRING SUBSTITUTION in the text, never re-serialised, so the CLI's own keys, ordering, escaping and indentation survive — a re-serialised document would be testing our formatting. A test asserts no redacted field is one the matcher reads. The logged-out case is recorded as NOT CAPTURED with its reason (capturing it means signing the Architect out of their own machine) and with the evidence standing in its place: the test flips the one boolean in the real document, and the text-mode denials were read out of the shipped binary (`Not logged in · Run /login`, `/^Not logged in$/`). A fixture invented and presented as a capture is worse than none, because the next reader trusts it.
- 2026-09-04 · (DEFECT CLEARANCE) **The Activity panel's opening race could restore the very bug the tail read fixed** · mounting starts `logTail`, and `onAppend` can fire before it answers — a company does not stop appending while a window opens. The handler then pulled with the cursor still at 0, so a forward page from the HEAD of the book raced the tail; answering second, it appended the company's first 300 rows and rewound the cursor to 300. Register item B4, arriving through the back door on the code path added to remove it. Fixed with TWO independent guarantees because either alone leaves a hole: `absorb` drops anything not newer than the cursor and never moves it backwards, and no forward page is issued until the tail lands (an append arriving first is remembered and collected afterwards). Each is killed by its own test, both orderings driven by a rig that can hold either read open.
- 2026-09-04 · (DEFECT CLEARANCE) **Seven mutants survived M8.3 and M8.4, and three of them were those packages' headline claims** · `grantsUnavailable` gutted to a constant `[]` passed every test in four files — that is M8.4's "the preview asks the spawn's resolver", asserted nowhere; deleting the dock's whole `notReady` branch, and separately its fix command, passed all seventeen cases in the dock's own file — that is M8.4's `needs-login` visibility; an adapter declaring NO probe read as logged out survived, because the existing case exercised the manager's missing injection rather than the adapter's missing declaration (Codex now covers it); the shipped 200 000-token spend ceiling could be changed to anything, so it is now asserted THROUGH `evaluateGate`, where an uncapped spend rule permits nothing; the Agora's publish snapshot survived because deleting from a Set during iteration is safe in JavaScript while ADDING is not, so the case that bites is a subscriber that arrives mid-delivery; and a degradation reported once rendered a redundant `×1`. Mutation score on the same forty-mutation set: 33/40 before, 40/40 after, plus 12/12 on the new code.
- 2026-09-04 · (ARCHITECT DECISION) **Branch protection on `main` applied, and the previous session's reason for not applying it was wrong** · the record said `gh api …/branches/main/protection` "still answers 404", read as a refusal. That 404 is GitHub's ordinary answer for **Branch not protected** — the absence of the setting, not a denial of it. The repository is public and the Architect holds admin; nothing had been blocking it for the whole M8 run, during which PR #6 merged over a red code job and three red suites skipped the coverage and reachability gates. `Typecheck · lint · test`, `Docs integrity` and `Commit attribution` are now required, with strict up-to-date branches, force-pushes and deletions refused, and conversation resolution required. `enforce_admins` is deliberately OFF: the Architect merges locally and pushes to `main`, and enforcing admins would force every change through a PR — what protection buys at this setting is that a MERGE cannot complete over a red check without an explicit recorded override. AUTOMATION.md carries the correction and the tick-list for verifying it in the web UI.
- 2026-09-04 · (M8.5, ARCHITECT DECISION) **The checkout is asked which repository it is; the screen shows the answer; the Architect may overrule it** · both shipped bundles carry `repos: []` and `harbor.json` was the only source of that list, so activating the Skeleton Crew against a real repository ingested nothing, raised no incident, and the whole `ingest → incident → Artemis → ledger` chain S-PROFILE proves could not START (B7). This machine only worked because `harbor.json` had been hand-edited. The Architect chose derivation WITH disclosure and an override over the two cheaper options: deriving silently (a wrong guess would have the company filing incidents against somebody else's repository) and asking the Architect to type it (a setup step on the flagship first-run path, which is the cliff M8.4 and M8.5 exist to remove). Precedence: what the Architect typed, then what the bundle declares, then what the remotes say, then nothing WITH THE REASON — and the reason names the consequence, `this instance will watch no repository, so no CI run, issue or pull request can reach it`. An empty override is not an override: "chose no repositories" and "did not choose" are different statements and only the second falls through.
- 2026-09-04 · (M8.5) **Refusing is a first-class answer, and a fork is the case it exists for** · a wrong slug is worse than no slug. `deriveRepo` refuses when the checkout's github remotes name more than one repository and NAMES BOTH — the fork case, where `origin` is the Architect's copy and `upstream` the canonical repository. Preferring `origin` was rejected explicitly: it would make a real decision silently and be right often enough that the times it was wrong would be baffling. Several remotes naming the SAME repository is not ambiguity (one answer written down twice) and resolves. A non-github remote beside a github one is not ambiguity either — only one of them is a repository this build can ingest from. Every refusal is a sentence written to be read by a human, and it says what to do about it (`name the one to watch`).
- 2026-09-04 · (M8.5) **A remote URL is never echoed, and a Windows drive letter is not a host** · `gh` itself writes remotes as `https://x-access-token:<token>@github.com/owner/repo.git`, so every refusal is built from parts and names the remote and its HOST only — ENGINEERING-STANDARDS §5, asserted in both directions (the answer and the refusal) with the token absent from each. And `C:\repos\myapp` matches the naive scp-shorthand pattern with host `c`: the Architect would have been told the target has "no github.com remote (c)" and gone looking for a host called `c`. The scp host must be at least two characters, which is git's own rule; a local path reports `not a URL` instead.
- 2026-09-04 · (M8.5) **`readRemotes` lives in `git.ts`, and `watchedRepos` replaced two inlined expressions** · ADR-0004's rule is about the single COMMITTER and this reads, but the invariant enforcing it is literal — no file but `git.ts` may invoke git — and a second file shelling out would defeat the check whatever it did. It takes a `GitRunner`, so it is injectable, and its tests run against REAL git in temp repositories plus one injected-runner case for the malformed lines git will not print on demand (without which every anchor in the parser is decoration). Separately, the Harbor's ingest list and the cadence's arming condition were two inlined expressions over the same data in `index.ts`; they are one exported `watchedRepos` now, for the M7.4 reason — an inlined resolver is untestable, so the only assertion available is a COPY that stays green while the original rots, which is exactly how the incident path lost its whole production life.
- 2026-09-04 · (M8.5) **`preview` became asynchronous rather than cached, and an inert instance is SAID rather than refused** · the remotes are read on every preview: a cached derivation is a setting nobody re-reads, which is the shape of every defect this milestone has found, and the Architect adds a remote between two activations like anybody else. A test drives that by changing the answer between two calls. And an activation whose derivation refused still comes up — refusing it outright would put a new cliff exactly where M8 is removing one, since a profile also hires a crew and arms schedules — but `onWatchesNothing` routes the condition to the degradation channel, and the screen says it in the warning colour before anything is activated. It was the silent outcome of every activation there had ever been.
- 2026-09-04 · (M8.5, A RATCHET THAT WENT TOO FAR, CORRECTED) **A floor is only a floor if the suite reproduces it** · the defect-clearance commit ratcheted `terraces.functions` from 84.65% to 85.15% on a single win32 measurement. Two later runs on unchanged terraces code both measured **84.65% exactly** (identical per-file figures: `vfx-art.ts` 0%, `FloorCanvas.tsx` 34.88%, `station-art.ts` 83.33%), so the gate then refused an M8.5 branch that had not touched a single file in that subsystem. The floor is restored to 84.65 — which is a CORRECTION of a bad ratchet, not the "lower the floor" move the M8.2 entry refused, and the distinction is that nothing under it changed. Recorded with its condition rather than as a bare number (n=3 on win32: 85.15 once, 84.65 twice), because a figure with no condition is not evidence. The lesson is about the tool, not this subsystem: `--update` ratchets whatever one run measured, and for a renderer subsystem whose covered functions depend on what a canvas managed to initialise, one run is not a measurement. Worth a guard in `check-coverage.cjs` — ratchet only what two runs agree on — and it is filed rather than built here because it is a gate change, not an M8.5 change.
- 2026-09-04 · (GATE, ARCHITECT DECISION) **The coverage gate's variable was never timing: it was the licensed art pack, and a floor must name every input that moves it** · three wrong answers in a row about one subsystem, each a different reading of a number without its condition. A ratchet on n=1 raised `terraces.functions` 84.65→85.15 (one function there is 0.495 points, TWICE the 0.25 tolerance); a hand "correction" back to 84.65 rested on four runs all in ONE checkout; and a follow-up session measured 84.16 three times in a `git worktree`, matched the linux record and called it "corroboration from a second machine". It was not. `src/renderer/src/assets/{tileset,characters}/*.png` are a deliberate gitignored drop (ATTRIBUTION.md rule 2 keeps assets whose licence forbids redistribution in source form out of the repository), so the globs are populated on a machine that bought the pack and EMPTY on every CI runner, fresh clone and worktree, permanently and by design. Empty globs mean both loader loops in `FloorCanvas` iterate zero times and `loadOne` is never called — measured by moving the pack aside and back: exactly 10 lines, 6 branches, 10 statements and 1 function of `terraces`, which is precisely the gap between 76.13/72.48/84.65/76.17 and 75.13/71.69/84.16/75.30. The two "independent" observers shared the condition rather than checking it. `treeHash` covers `src/**/*.{ts,tsx}` and `shims/**/*.mjs` only, so both checkouts stamped tree `c08617b1335b` while measuring different subjects.
- 2026-09-04 · (GATE) **The variable is removed rather than recorded, and the art path now runs in CI for the first time** · the alternative — teaching `check-coverage.cjs` that asset presence is a condition key — is honest about reality but institutionalises two win32 conditions, doubles the floors to maintain, and leaves the loader path untested forever. `test/renderer/floor-canvas.test.tsx` now stubs both glob modules, calling the REAL `resolveTileset`/`resolveCharacters` over fixed inputs rather than hand-building a state literal (the M8.4 lesson one package later: a literal is our idea of the shape and drifts the moment the schema moves). Proven both ways on this machine: `FloorCanvas` 121/331 lines and 29/48 functions WITH the pack installed and 121/331, 29/48 with it moved aside — identical. The second consequence is the one that outranks the floors: `loadOne`, both loader loops and the sheet-error arm had never once run in CI. They were exercised on exactly one machine on earth, which is a seam-rule defect (ENGINEERING-STANDARDS §6.7) independent of any floor and would have survived every correct floor decision.
- 2026-09-04 · (GATE) **The corroboration guard is kept; the floors it was calibrated against are not** · a raise now takes three runs of one tree and rises only to their metric-wise MINIMUM, which is the correct fix for the n=1 ratchet and whose 47 cases are sound. Its four hand-lowered win32 floors are NOT taken: they sit exactly 10 covered lines below what a with-pack machine reproduces, so `loadOne` and both loops could stop being covered altogether and the gate would still say ok — and three `--update` runs on a with-pack machine would raise them back, after which the next pack-free run hard-fails at −1.00 against a 0.25 tolerance, which is the same trap re-armed. Floors are left UNRATCHETED: the stub raises coverage, so every floor is now conservative rather than wrong, and raising it takes three corroborating runs, which is the process working. Two further corrections to the guard: the subsystem map is compared by MEMBERSHIP rather than by its set of names (moving a directory between two existing subsystems left both names standing and folded straight through — a minimum across two subjects), proven by BYPASS 10 and by two killed mutations; and a comment describing a `MAX_WINDOW_RUNS` restart the code does not perform is corrected to say the window slides.
- 2026-09-04 · (GATE, GYMNASIUM) **GYM-006's metric 2 was right in its instruction and too narrow in its diagnosis** · it reads "a platform-jitter failure counts as a false positive and triggers a re-measured tolerance, never a lowered floor". The instruction held and was violated twice. The diagnosis assumed the only cross-machine variable is OS timing; it was an undeclared build input the tool is structurally incapable of seeing. Amended to require that a per-platform floor NAMES EVERY INPUT that changes it, with the false-positive count recorded honestly: one, and its cause was not jitter. Filed as GYM-007 with its own row rather than folded in, because the change carries an operational cost that must be MEASURED and could plausibly fail: after this, raising a linux floor needs three `--update --from` applications of artifacts from three CI runs on one unchanged production tree, and a change whose failure mode is "the record silently stops being ratchetable" is exactly what the ledger's measured-outcome column exists for. ENGINEERING-STANDARDS §3 makes the entry blocking, not optional: "altered CI gates without a ledger entry are defects, whoever makes them."
- 2026-09-04 · (GATE, THE FULL ACCOUNT — TWO SESSIONS, TWO CAUSES) **Every figure the terraces record ever held is one of four combinations of two independent binary conditions** · both sessions working this reached for a SINGLE cause and each found one of the two, which is why it took two passes. **A**: the licensed art pack is present, so `loadOne` is called at all — worth +1 function and +10 lines. **B**: the bring-up has settled, so `loadOne`'s `.catch` arm runs — worth +1 function and +1 line. Against the pre-change denominators (202 functions, 997 lines): functions 170 = 84.16 (neither — a pack-free worktree and CI linux), 171 = 84.65 (A only — this checkout before `onBringUp`), 172 = 85.15 (both); lines 749 = 75.13, 759 = 76.13, 760 = 76.23 respectively. Nothing is left over, and the original n=1 ratchet to 85.15 was not luck in a vague sense — it was the run in which a SECOND binary condition also happened to hold. The generalisation is GYM-007's: a floor names every input that moves it, and "I found the cause" is a claim worth one more look when a number has more than one way to move.
- 2026-09-04 · (GATE) **One line of `loadOne` cannot be covered under jsdom, and is recorded as such rather than left as a gap** · counting the loader region out of `coverage-final.json` gives ELEVEN uncovered lines without the pack, not ten. Read back from `coverage-final.json` with the stub in place, `loadOne`, `loadSheets` and `loadCharacters` are all ENTERED and exactly THREE lines in that region stay uncovered — 734 `if (texture) loaded.set(name, texture)`, 742 its tilesheet twin, and 751 `return new Texture({ … })` — all three for one reason: jsdom rejects `image.decode()` on a real PNG exactly as it does on a missing one, so `loadOne` always takes its catch and always returns null, whether or not the pack is installed and whether or not the globs are stubbed. The peer session identified 751; checking it here showed the same mechanism accounts for the other two. Written down so a future reader of that figure does not hunt the twelfth line: covering it needs a decoder, which is a different ENVIRONMENT, not a different test. The file's own docblock has said since M6 that Pixi cannot initialise under jsdom and that this is deliberate rather than worked around; this is the same boundary, one layer down.
- 2026-09-04 · (GATE, THE RESIDUAL, SETTLED BY MEASUREMENT) **The last 0.27 of branch difference was two arms, and both were a licence credit nothing tested** · after the art-pack stub, terraces agreed across win32-with-pack and linux CI to the hundredth on lines, functions and statements, and still differed by 0.27 on branches. Percentages could not locate it — they are what hid the whole episode — so: two full `npm run test:coverage` runs on one tree, pack on and pack off, keeping `coverage-final.json` from each, then diffing every `(branchId, arm)` whose count is zero in one and non-zero in the other. Exactly TWO arms, one in each resolver, nothing else in the subsystem moving (`FloorCanvas` read 129/364 lines and 33/178 branches in BOTH runs): `src/shared/characters.ts:182` and `src/shared/tileset.ts:284`, both the `credit === undefined ? '' : …` ternary. Every fixture in `tileset.test.ts` and `characters-intake.test.ts` omits `credit`; every shipped manifest — `limezu.chars.json` and all three `*.tiles.json` — carries one. So the credit side ran ONLY on a machine holding the licensed pack and never in CI. `src/shared/tileset.ts:281` says why that matters, immediately above the line: "The credit rides the same line as the name: a licence term nobody can see is a licence term nobody is honouring." UI-DESIGN §7 makes credits mandatory and the note is the only place one is shown, so the code honouring a licence obligation was exercised by accident on one machine and by nothing deterministic anywhere — the same defect class as `loadOne`. Fixed with a credit-bearing fixture case in each resolver; both arms now run from fixtures alone with no glob and no pack involved (verified arms=[1,1] and arms=[9,1] from those two files by themselves). **The transferable part is the method:** when a figure moves with an undeclared input, diff the two conditions at ARM level. Two sessions spent three wrong diagnoses comparing percentages.

- 2026-09-04 · (M8.6, ARCHITECT DECISION) **Isolation is declared in three layers, and the default is `worktree`** · The profile spawn path never asked for isolation at all, so every hire in both shipped bundles ran git operations and file edits concurrently in the Architect's own working copy — the one register item that can destroy uncommitted work. The composition mirrors `composeAutonomyTable` deliberately: hire template → profile document → built-in default, then the Architect's per-activation choice (`as-declared` / `isolate-all` / `none`), then a clamp for a target with no repository to make a worktree of. Rejected: **hire template only** (changing it for one run means editing a versioned bundle, and there is no disclosure of an override because there are no overrides); **implied by capability** (makes the capability list a security boundary it was never designed to be — a new capability string would silently change isolation); **a harness-wide switch** (cannot express "the improver is isolated, the read-only docs agent is not", which FR-9.5 requires). The built-in default is `worktree` rather than `target` because "declares nothing" describes both shipped bundles for their entire production life; a bare `agents.spawn` under UC-01, where a person typed the directory and confirmed one agent, keeps the schema's optional-false default. Whether the worktree can actually be made is deliberately NOT pre-checked on the screen: the create is the truth, and a preview that says "ok" before a `git worktree add` that fails is two code paths that can disagree (the M8.5 lesson, applied before it cost anything).

- 2026-09-04 · (M8.6, ARCHITECT DECISION) **A spawn that asks for isolation and cannot have it is REFUSED** · `isolate()` used to log the failure and continue in the Architect's checkout, on the reasoning recorded in the code that "isolation is a nicety, and refusing to hire over it would be worse than saying so". That reasoning is inverted: the fallback IS the harm — an agent that asked to be kept out of somebody's working copy and was silently put into it is precisely the outcome isolation is requested to prevent, and it is the silent degradation BUILD-PROMPT §3 forbids, since the Architect learns of it from a log line after the writes. The spawn now throws with git's own reason and releases the claimed agent id first, so the Architect can fix the repository and activate again without a phantom agent holding the name; `respawn` does the same and leaves the card `exited`. Rejected: **keep the fallback with a louder degradation** (the disclosure arrives after the risk was taken — overnight, that means the morning) and **fall back read-only** (not enforceable — the harness cannot stop a CLI writing files, only decline to grant it, so it would be an assertion the code cannot keep, which is the defect class M8 exists to remove).

- 2026-09-04 · (M8.6, ARCHITECT DECISION) **A rung-3 breaker stop outlives the process it stopped** · `forget` ran on every exit including the exit rung 3 had just caused, so an exhausted budget cycled instead of stopping: 21 climbs to rung 1 against exactly one completed rung-3 stop across a 24.9M-token day. The non-obvious part is why keeping the RUNG would have fixed nothing — spans are session state and go with the process, so the next sweep sees nothing firing and `nextRung(3, false)` returns 0 in one step; the change would have looked right and done nothing. What survives is the DECISION: a `BreakerStop` record, written BEFORE the stop is performed (the process is about to exit and `onChange` will call `forgetSession`, so a record written after that race is a record nobody can see), refusing every respawn path until a human clears it. One verb became two — `forgetSession` (an exit: drops spans and rung, keeps the stop) and `forgetAgent` (decommissioning: drops everything) — because the difference between "this process ended" and "this agent is finished" is the whole of B11. Rejected: **persist to disk now** (M8.8's scope; the record is serializable and single-owner precisely so that lands as wiring rather than a redesign).

- 2026-09-04 · (M8.6, ARCHITECT DECISION) **A hire declares what happens when it dies; the ladder is shared** · 46 `respawn-scheduled` rows in one day, every one of them Artemis, while three crew agents logged terminal exits four, five and five times and nothing brought any of them back. FR-5.4's backoff ladder was correct and had exactly one user. `RespawnOffer` had been computed on every exit since M3 and had ZERO references outside the main process — SDD §10's "respawn offer on the agent card" had never been rendered. Both halves land: the offer is surfaced with an `agents.respawn` IPC, and a hire may declare `onExit: "respawn"` to get the same ladder automatically. Default stays `offer`, SDD §10's own word, so an omitted field keeps documented behaviour. Rejected: **offer only** (a crash at 03:00 means a dead crew until morning, which is the thing this milestone is named after) and **automatic for all crew** (no way to say "this one should not come back", and a broken brief becomes a crash loop spending budget on the ladder; the bundle stops being where an Architect reads what may happen). The ladder was EXTRACTED from `artemis.ts` rather than copied — a second copy would duplicate the parts that are actually hard (the pending-promise handoff, the hold, the stability reset) — with Artemis's own 653-line suite as the regression net, green unchanged at every step.

- 2026-09-04 · (M8.6) **The orchestrator is not exempt from her own stop** · Not in the package as written; it surfaced from the coverage pass, which exposed a `respawn-blocked` log branch that could never fire because nothing wired Artemis a `blocked` predicate. If the breaker stops her at rung 3 and her ladder immediately undoes it, rung 3 is a pause rather than a rung — B11's cycle with a laurel wreath. FR-14.5 already treats a rung-3 stop on her work as consequential enough to revert the company's mode. Asked at the LADDER rather than only inside `AgentManager.respawn`, so a blocked exit costs no rung: five refusals in a row would otherwise end the ladder and report "crashed 5 times" about an agent that never crashed.

- 2026-09-04 · (M8.6) **The coverage gate caught boot dilution, and the fix was extraction, not a lower floor** · The first draft put the `CrewSurvival` construction — the log mapping, the degradation causes, the "still down" reading — inline in `index.ts`. `boot` failed its floor on the dilution alone: `index.ts` is 0% covered, so ~23 added executable lines moved the subsystem below its ratchet. That is the gate working. The fix followed M8.1's own precedent (which moved `shutdown.ts` and `ui-bridge.ts` out of the same file for the same reason): every one of those is a DECISION, and decisions belong where a test can reach them, so they moved into `createCrewSurvival` and `respawnBlockReason` in `respawn.ts` and `index.ts` kept only wiring. `boot` returned above its floor without the floor moving, and `artemis` and `engines` rose. Recording this because the two peer branches carrying hand-lowered win32 floors show what the other answer looks like.

- 2026-09-04 · (M8.6, REFUTATION) **Two of 28 mutations survived the first pass, and both were the same shape** · Per the standing rule that a new gate is refuted before it closes: 28 mutations against the rules this package introduces, each run against the suites claiming to catch it. Two survived. (1) Deleting the rung-3 guard inside `AgentManager.respawn` killed nothing — both ladders ask `blocked` before scheduling, so the guard covering the path with NO ladder, the Architect pressing "bring it back", had no test; that is the path SDD §10 specifies and the one this package built the IPC for. (2) Hard-coding `blockedBecause: null` killed nothing — the dock tests built the offer by hand, so nothing asserted `AgentManager` fills it from the predicate. **The transferable shape: a rule enforced only where something else already enforces it is a check that cannot fail.** Both were closed in `test/main/agents.test.ts`; the second pass killed 28/28.

- 2026-09-05 · (SURVIVAL FOLLOW-UP, ARCHITECT REQUEST) **Rung-3 decisions survive app restarts and have an explicit recovery control.** The request to close the survival audit's remaining gaps adds app-local `breaker-stops.json` (schema 1, validated and atomically replaced), startup enforcement in both `spawn` and `respawn`, and Watch's `breakerStops()` / `clearBreakerStop(agentId, expectedAt)` IPC. A stale clear is refused; clearing persists before lifting constraints and refreshes the card without emitting a lifecycle exit or spawning. Corrupt storage blocks starts and stays visible. See `docs/implementations/2026-09-05-durable-breaker-stops.md` for the decision, evidence and storage-failure boundaries.

- 2026-09-05 · (M8.7, ADR-0025) **Workspace trust follows the agent into its worktree** · Found by an adversarial audit of M8.6, not by a test, and the reason no test could find it is the finding: nothing related the directory the trust record NAMES to the directory the agent is spawned INTO. `claude-trust.test.ts` only ever passed a bare directory; no activation test opened `.claude.json`. The defect: ADR-0021 pre-trusts `request.target.path`, M8.6 then made every profile hire work in `<home>/worktrees/<agentId>`, and Claude Code matches its trust key on the exact resolved path — so no crew agent's real working directory was ever trusted, every one of them met the first-run dialog (highlighted default `No, exit`) before any session existed, and therefore with no hook to report it. That is the live MUSAHIT parking failure ADR-0021 was written to close, re-entered from the other side by isolation. The fix stays at activation time because ADR-0021 forbids the obvious wiring by name — "never from spawn, respawn, or a wake", with "pre-trust at spawn" listed as a rejected option — so `ProfileActivations` gained a `beforeHires(plan)` seam that runs after the plan is fixed and before the first process. **The anti-drift property is the whole fix:** `plannedWorkspaces(plan, worktreePathFor)` reads the SAME plan object the hires spawn from and asks the SAME path function the lifecycle spawns with, because a trusted path one character off from the used path is a record nothing reads whose only symptom is a hung agent. `index.ts` had computed the worktree root in three independent places; it now has one. Rejected: trusting `<home>/worktrees` once (the engine matches the exact key, established by experiment in ADR-0021, so it covers no child); pre-creating the directory so it could be resolved in full (`Worktrees.create` refuses a path that exists, which would break isolation itself); narrowing the target grant to activations that actually put a hire there (least-privilege and tempting, but that CHANGES ADR-0021 rather than extending it and is not needed here — recorded as the obvious next narrowing).

- 2026-09-05 · (M8.7) **`realpath` cannot guard a directory that does not exist, so it guards the parent** · The junction protection ADR-0021 rests on — resolve the path so the record names the directory actually approved, not a link repointed afterwards — cannot run against a worktree git has not created yet. `WorkspaceExistence` makes the two cases explicit rather than silently weakening one: `must-exist` (the default, so every pre-M8.7 caller and test is bit-identical) resolves the whole path; `will-be-created` resolves the PARENT and appends the leaf. The guard is not lost where it can matter — the parent is `<home>/worktrees`, which the harness creates and owns, and the leaf is a name derived from an agent id, so nothing a target repository or a third party controls is left unresolved. A test asserts the two modes produce the SAME key for the same directory once it exists, which is the property that makes the record findable at all. A mutation flipping the default to `will-be-created` is killed by its own test — that direction would quietly weaken every existing caller.

- 2026-09-05 · (M8.7, METHOD) **CRLF sources silently defeat multi-line mutation anchors** · Cost two full refutation passes today. `src/**` in this repository is CRLF; a mutation harness that anchors on a string spanning a line boundary with `\n` never matches, and the harness reports NOT-APPLIED — which reads at a glance like a mutation that was tried, not one that was skipped. Five of ten M8.7 mutations silently did not run the first time, including the one that reproduces the original blocker. **Use single-line anchors, and treat NOT-APPLIED as a failure of the harness rather than a result.** The same lesson applies to any scripted edit of this tree.

- 2026-09-05 · (M8.7) **The quit sequence gained a DISARM phase, because a comment is not an ordering** · M8.6 registered `crew.stop()` among the quit's `steps`, with a comment reading "Before the unwind, not after: a ladder still armed treats the shutdown's own kills as crashes and respawns the company it is tearing down." The comment described the intent exactly and the code did the opposite: `QuitSequence.execute` runs `runClosing` → `runAgents` (the unwind) → `runSteps`, so `steps` is the LAST phase. Every respawn ladder was armed while the unwind killed the agents it was watching. Found by the M8.6 audit; confirmed by reading `execute` rather than the comment beside the step. **Why nothing caught it: no test related the phase a step is REGISTERED in to the phase it actually RUNS in** — the ordering test asserted `ask → closing → unwind → stops` and the crew step was simply one of the stops. Fixed with a `disarm()` seam that runs between closing time and the unwind, isolated exactly as `steps` is (a ladder that will not disarm is reported and stepped over, because it must not cost the unwind its settings restores) and with its own degradation cause (`shutdown/disarm:<name>`) so a wedged ladder does not read as a wedged teardown stop. `Artemis.stop()` went into the same phase: it had **zero production callers**, so FR-5.4's ladder brought the orchestrator back on every exit including the one the quit itself performs. Five mutations; the three semantic ones killed, including the original defect (the two survivors were deliberate no-op rewrites used as anchor controls).

- 2026-09-05 · (M8.7, METHOD) **Line endings are mixed in this tree, and that silently breaks scripted edits** · `src/shared/profile-activation.ts` and `src/main/profiles.ts` are CRLF; `src/main/shutdown.ts` is LF. A scripted patch or mutation anchored on a string spanning a line boundary matches in one file and silently fails in the other — and a mutation harness reports NOT-APPLIED, which reads at a glance like a mutation that ran rather than one that was skipped. It cost two full refutation passes in one day, and five of ten mutations silently did not run the first time INCLUDING the one reproducing the blocker under test. Rules: single-line anchors; check the file's endings before a multi-line patch; treat NOT-APPLIED as a harness failure, never as a result; and put a deliberate no-op mutation in every batch as a live-anchor control.

- 2026-09-06 · (ARCHITECT REQUEST, FR-11.7) **Both company ceilings become a setting, and the writer refuses rather than replacing a policy it cannot read** · The autonomy ceiling has clamped since ADR-0012 and the budget one since M8.9, and neither was reachable except by hand-editing `~/.ephesus/gate-policy.json` while the app ran — the threat model told operators to do exactly that, twice (§6.3, §7), which is an admission that the safety story depended on an undocumented text edit. `SettingsPanel` puts both in the Watch. **The non-obvious part is the writer.** `loadGatePolicy` never widens on failure: an unreadable file reads as `denyAllPolicy`, whose `rules` table is EMPTY. A read-modify-write over what the loader returns would therefore take the Architect's six gate rules, drop every one, and write the result back as though it had been chosen — a transient read error becoming permanent data loss, at the exact moment somebody was adjusting a safety dial. So `saveGateCeilings` re-reads the file itself and REFUSES when it is missing or invalid; a save is a patch of a valid policy, never a replacement of an unreadable one. Two narrower decisions sit under it: `rules` is not reachable from the renderer at all (`gateCeilingsSchema` is strict, so sending it is refused rather than ignored — a click that could widen `needs-human` is not a setting), and `maxDailyTokens: null` DELETES the key rather than writing a figure, because on disk unbudgeted is the absence of a ceiling (ADR-0029) and a zero reads as breached before the first token. The delete was written first as a conditional spread and was wrong — a spread can only ADD the key back, so turning the ceiling off silently did nothing; the test caught it. Rejected: a separate `settings.json` (a second truth for one policy), a top-level SETTINGS tab (`SecretsPanel` set the panel-within-the-Watch precedent, and the ceilings belong beside the spend they bound), and seeding a missing policy from the panel (inventing a policy on the one path where the Architect cannot see what they are agreeing to).

- 2026-09-06 · (METHOD) **Two of three mutation survivors were equivalent, and the fix was deleting the duplication rather than writing a test** · The ceilings work ended 14/17, and neither survivor was a missing test. (1) `parseGatePolicy(next)` before the write could not fail: both halves were already validated, and the only way they could combine into something invalid was if the file schema's and the wire schema's bounds drifted apart — and the duplicate was the literal `1_000_000_000` written twice. Extracting `maxDailyTokensSchema` for both uses made the guard provably redundant, so it was deleted. (2) `unbudgeted ? null : wanted` could not disagree with `wanted`, which is already null whenever the company is unbudgeted; the ternary went. The third survivor was a **broken mutant, not a result**: it referenced a symbol `gates.ts` does not import, the ReferenceError landed in the try/catch it was meant to defeat, and the mutant returned the same refusal it was supposed to remove. Second pass: 15/15. **Read a survivor before believing it — an equivalent mutant is a design smell and a broken mutant is a measurement that did not happen; neither is a test to write.**

- 2026-09-06 · (METHOD) **A coverage floor is a measurement of a tree, so attribute the drop before you chase it** · The seam rule failed `engines` and `hermes` on a change that touches neither. Blaming every uncovered line against the floor's own recorded commit (`bf0c087`, 2026-09-05 — the record stores the condition, which is the point) showed exactly ONE uncovered line post-dated it: the rest was denominator movement from a day of committed work. Not a new blind spot, and not something to ratchet away either — `--update` never lowers a floor by design. Closing the 6 lines was still worth doing because of what it surfaced: two Hermes bounces (a message for an endpoint nobody is listening on must be returned, never dropped) and four `resolveToolGrants` refusals had never been shown to fire, and `transcriptExists` was documented "never throws" with the adapter call sitting OUTSIDE its try. Two more branches turned out to be genuinely unreachable and were left recorded rather than tested (`commands.ts` line 154, `tool-grants.ts`'s empty-relative case). **The percentage was the prompt; the dead guards were the finding.**

- 2026-09-06 · (M8.8 AUDIT, ADR-0030) **The audit's method was ADR-0027's own test, and it found exactly one map that fails it** · Working toward a releasable MVP, M8.8 was re-checked by execution rather than by re-reading its evidence block. What it built holds up — `restore.ts`, `state-store.ts` and `restart.ts` are at 100% lines, S-BLACKOUT restarts over two real lifetimes, all three subsystems persist from a PRIVATE method so no caller can forget one, and save failures are reported and cleared. The productive part was applying ADR-0027's rule (*persist a decision about an identity; not an observation about a process; not what a live subsystem re-derives*) to every remaining in-memory map in `src/main/`. Exactly one fails it: `FrontOffice.held`. `gates.json` restored an `outbound` gate and nothing restored the DRAFT it held, so the Architect approved a gate whose packaging read perfectly, `onVerdict` returned `false`, and `index.ts` discarded it — the comment never left the machine and nothing said so. **M8.8 did not cause it; it made it REACHABLE**, because before the restore the queue came back empty and the gate could not be approved at all. Closed with a fourth record following the same per-subsystem pattern, a boot reconcile that REPORTS a draftless gate rather than settling it (ADR-0027 §4's rule, same reasoning), and the discarded `false` turned into a degradation. `FrontOffice.held` was the only gate-keyed payload outside `GateManager`, so the class is closed rather than one instance.

- 2026-09-06 · (METHOD) **Two mutation survivors in a row changed the design instead of the tests** · The drafts record went 8/9, then 10/11, then 11/11, and neither survivor was a test to write. (1) *"a verdict does not update the record"* survived because `restore()` re-held every draft with a non-null `gateId` — so a DECIDED draft came back pending whether or not the persist ran, and the standup would have put an answered comment back in front of the Architect. That is where the `awaiting` field came from; the mutant found a defect the fix had not addressed. (2) *"a hold with no gate still claims to be awaiting one"* was **equivalent**: with `gateId === null` nothing reads `awaiting`, because both `held.set` and `restore` are already guarded on the gate id. Two fields that cannot disagree, so the relationship moved into the schema (`awaiting` implies a gate) where `JsonStateStore.save` refuses the bad pair before writing — and the mutant became killable. **Read the survivor before patching the test: one was a missing design, the other was a missing constraint, and neither was a missing assertion.**

- 2026-09-06 · (M8.7 AUDIT, ADR-0031) **A capability the harness cannot deliver has to be DECLARED, not assumed** · The audit asked whether M8.7's two claims — engine isolation and the autonomy hinge — hold for every engine Ephesus ships, and established by execution that they hold for one. `claude` maps the composed level onto `--permission-mode` and runs in its own `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR`; `codex` and `gemini` carry no autonomy signal anywhere in argv or env and redirect no config directory. **The two halves compound:** with the operator's own config in play, a company set to `manual` could run a codex hire under a never-ask policy while the activation screen, the agent card and the new Ceilings panel all reported `manual`. The tree already had the right shape for this — `HookSupport` — and autonomy simply never got one. So `autonomySupport` joins the adapter contract with TWO grades and deliberately no middle one (a partial mapping is the dangerous shape: it looks enforced and holds for some levels), the declaration is conformance-checked in BOTH directions, and a hire stricter than `autonomous` on a `none` engine is refused at spawn. `autonomous` passes, because an engine being stricter of its own accord costs a stalled turn rather than an unpermitted action. Rejected: mapping the two CLIs now (their flags are not established here, and the standing rule is that engine behaviour is RUN before it is written down — ADR-0026's own method); un-registering them (removes a capability to fix a disclosure problem); warning instead of refusing (a warning beside a wrong number is still a wrong number); clamping to `autonomous` (silently loosening a ceiling is the one direction ADR-0012 forbids).

- 2026-09-06 · (M8.7 AUDIT, METHOD) **The observation the register said was missing was sitting in the harness home all along** · M8.7 closed with "the end-to-end claim rests on the key-equality test rather than observation". It took one command to settle: every crew agent's isolated `.claude.json` carries two trusted keys — the target repo and that agent's OWN worktree — those worktrees exist, and `agent.artemis`'s record holds real cost and token counts, so the engine demonstrably wrote into the isolated config rather than the operator's. The operator's own `~/.claude.json` has 111 project keys and not one agent worktree among them. **A claim recorded as unobservable was observable from a run that had already happened** — when a package defers its end-to-end evidence, check the artefacts the real runs left behind before assuming the gap is still open.

- 2026-09-06 · (METHOD) **A conformance check that mints a fresh fixture per call measures the fixture** · The autonomy-grade check renders one spawn plan at `manual` and one at `autonomous` and compares them. Written with a `conformanceRig()` call inside the render helper, it compared plans from two different temp directories, so `--cd` differed and EVERY adapter looked autonomy-sensitive — including the two the check was written to catch, which "failed" for the right reason by accident and would have "passed" once fixed the wrong way. One rig, both levels, vary only the variable under test.

- 2026-09-06 · (M8.6 AUDIT) **Three of four decisions verified from the machine, not the suite** · The durable rung-3 stop is not just stored, it FIRED: `breaker-stops.json` holds a crew agent's stop and the log shows it refusing a later reactivation with `"will not be respawned — the breaker stopped it at rung 3 (burn-rate); clear the stop first"`. Crew respawns went from the register's measured zero to `ci-babysitter` x5 and `verifier` x3. And the one crew agent with an engine config but no worktree turned out to be that same stopped agent, its worktree released on death — the feature working, not a leak. What was left was small: `git worktree remove` unregistered three worktrees and left their EMPTY directories behind, unknown to `git worktree list`. `Worktrees.remove` now sweeps an empty leftover and reports one that still holds files rather than deleting it — the same rule that keeps `--force` out of that module. Bounded before the fix only because `worktreePathIsVacant` had learned that morning that an empty directory is vacant; before THAT, the residue of one activation refused the next for the same agent id.

- 2026-09-06 · (METHOD, a wrong finding worth keeping) **Surveying by one field and concluding absence** · The M8.6 audit reported that the crew's spawn was missing from the book of record — no `event: 'spawned'` row for any crew agent, so a forensic reader could not answer whether a hire ran in its own worktree or in the Architect's checkout, which is the exact hazard the package names. It was **wrong**. `kind: 'spawn'` rows carry no `event` field at all, so a survey grouping by `event` cannot see them: there are 132 recording `cwd` for every spawn, isolated or not, and 51 more carrying `worktree`, `branch`, `branchCreated` and the source repo. The record was complete and the proposed fix would have added a second, worse copy of it. **Before reporting that something is not recorded, group the log by SHAPE and not by the field you expected** — `Counter(tuple(sorted(row.keys())))` found it in one command. The same mistake in the other direction is what the audit had just praised M8.8 for avoiding.

- 2026-09-06 · (M8.9 SCOPE AUDIT, nothing built) **A third of the package had already landed, and the register did not know** · M8.9 was written on 2026-09-02 naming three items. One of them — the `feature/usage-aware-pacing` merge (`9d66df5`), described as UNMERGED and structurally conflicting with the capacity UI — is reachable from HEAD with its content in place: the dock imports `paceNoteOf`, calls `eph.watch.usage()`, and carries the capacity row beside the pace strip, with renderer tests on both halves. So *"the pace verdict never reaches the renderer at all"* stopped being true at some point nobody recorded. **An unchecked package is not automatically an unbuilt one** — audit the register against the tree before planning from it, or a build session spends its first hour re-deciding a merge that already happened. B15 is partly done for the same reason (the pace half came with that merge); what is genuinely open is the bridge check being one-shot at mount and there being no heartbeat. B14 has not been started and is larger than a panel: `IncidentEndpoint` reaches the world only through `onLogEvent`, so there is no durable incident store to render — ADR-0027's question (held, or derived?) has to be answered before anything is drawn.

- 2026-09-06 · (M8.9 AUDIT, TWO LIVE DEFECTS) **A refusal that cannot teach the rule it enforces** · Reading the data B14 would have surfaced: 21 incident triage attempts on the Architect's machine, **12 refused**, nine of them `agent.artemis` replying in prose to `agent.harbor`. She is doing what `prompts/harbor/incident-body.md` asks — open the task, assign it — and then writing back to say so, which the SAME prompt forbids by name and warns will bounce. It bounced nine times. The guard is correct and the message is not: she is told `triage report: not JSON — Unexpected token 'T'`, when the answer she needs is "you were not asked to reply here". **A parse error cannot teach the rule it is enforcing**, and a refusal an agent cannot learn from will be re-earned every time. Second defect, same reading: 3 of 6 root-cause verdicts discarded with `Too big: expected string to have <=2000 characters` (`src/shared/root-cause.ts:141`) — a verifier's reasoning lost to a length cap, refused without naming the limit. Both are refusal-quality work (invariant §8, the words are a prompt surface), both are independent of the panel, and both are live waste today. Recorded for the build session rather than fixed, at the Architect's direction.

- 2026-09-07 · (M8.5 AUDIT) **The algorithm's half of a hazard was tested fifty ways; the human's half was untestable** · M8.5 exists because guessing which repository a checkout is can file this company's incidents against somebody else's. `deriveRepo` refuses to guess between a fork's two remotes, and that refusal is tested exhaustively — the parse, the precedence table, the credential position on both parse paths, the Windows drive letter, S-PROFILE end to end. The OVERRIDE is how the Architect answers that refusal, and the rule that stops their answer following them to a different checkout — the same wrong-repository outcome, reached by a human instead of an algorithm — sat in a `useCallback` that `renderToStaticMarkup` cannot run. Nothing referenced it. **Look for the hazard's other half: where a guard refuses to decide, something else decides, and that something is usually less tested.** Fixed by extracting `overrideAfterRetarget`, which is the lesson `parseRepoList` in the same file was extracted for and which this package's own test docblock states.

- 2026-09-07 · (METHOD) **Extracting a rule for testability does not test that anything still calls it** · After `overrideAfterRetarget` was pulled out and table-tested, five of six mutations died. The survivor removed the panel's CALL to it entirely and killed nothing: the static-markup harness runs no handler, so the rule was reachable by tests and, as far as any test could tell, by nothing else. That is M6's lesson in miniature — 1406 lines nothing could reach — arriving through a refactor meant to fix testability. A jsdom test now mounts the panel, types an override, moves the target and asserts the box cleared. **When a mutation pass covers an extraction, mutate the CALL SITE as well as the callee**, or the extraction has moved the untested part rather than removed it.

- 2026-09-07 · (M8.4 AUDIT) **A package's own defect recurred because the fix was an edit, not a check** · M8.4's line named it: "the README has no setup section and its status is two milestones stale". It wrote both. By M8.9 the same section was three packages behind — M8.6, M8.7a/b and M8.8 absent, `worktree` 0 hits, `restart` 0, `respawn` 0 across the whole file — so the front door described a company without worktree isolation, without engine isolation and without a survivable restart. **Fixing an instance of a drift defect leaves the defect.** The prose is now written AND `scripts/check-readme-current.cjs` fails CI when a package ticked in PROGRESS is missing from the README's `<!-- landed: -->` marker. It compares one claim against another and deliberately does not read the prose: deciding whether a paragraph "describes" a package is not something a script can do, and a check that guessed would either block good writing or pass bad writing, both worse than no check. It catches the oversight that happened twice — a package landing while nobody touched the README — and says in its own header what it cannot catch. Scoped to the milestone holding the most recently ticked package, because "Landed so far" is one milestone's narrative and requiring every package since M0 would ask the front door to be the register.

- 2026-09-07 · (METHOD) **Scripted edits keep writing control characters where escapes belong** · Third time today. A `\u0000` in a generated test became a literal NUL byte and turned the file binary; a `\b` in a generated regex became a literal backspace (0x08) and the check silently matched nothing while exiting 0 — which is the dangerous one, because a checker that finds nothing looks exactly like a checker that passes. Both survived a green test run. The pattern is always the same: a Python heredoc parses the escape before the target file ever sees it. **Build the character in the target language (`String.fromCharCode(0)`), or write the byte pair explicitly (`bytes([92]) + b'b'`) — and after generating any file with escapes in it, grep the line back before trusting a pass.**

- 2026-09-07 · (M8.3 AUDIT) **The register claimed a guarantee, and a test three files away asserted its opposite** · M8.3's evidence says `logRowSummary` is "unable to return an empty string". `test/shared/log-row.test.ts` contained `expect(logRowSummary(entry('gate'))).toBe('')` under the title *has nothing to say only when the entry itself carries nothing*. Both were written for the same package. Three of four degenerate shapes rendered blank, and `{kind:'breaker', signals:[]}` rendered `"rung "` — a bare label, which is the same looks-populated-says-nothing failure this very package fixed for `signal` vs `signals`. Latent rather than live: 2,689 real rows render with zero blanks. But log rows come from DISK, so an older format, a hand-edit or a future object-shaped payload reaches the Activity panel as an empty line, which IS B3. **When a package's prose states a guarantee, grep for the test that pins it — the absence of one is the finding, and a test asserting the opposite is a louder one.** Fixed with a `labelled()` helper (seven sites unified; `exit` and `rung` were the two guarding their value ad hoc or not at all) and a final fallback naming kind and seq; the contradicting test was reversed with the reason in its body, per M8.4's precedent.

- 2026-09-07 · (M8.3 AUDIT) **A default that is wrong for every caller who does not think about it** · `readLog(afterSeq = 0, limit = 500)` — the oldest five hundred entries — IS B3. M8.3 moved the three production callers onto `readLogSince`/`readLogAll` and left the default in place for the next one. Both arguments are now required, which turned up **57 test call sites taking it**: every one of them meant "the log" and every fixture was smaller than the window, which is the same sentence this package wrote about why a green suite never saw the defect. They now call `readLogAll()`. Two contrast cases were deliberately NOT converted — they exist to demonstrate the window — and pass `readLog(0, 500)` explicitly, which the blanket replacement had broken and the suite caught. **Removing a trap tells you who was standing in it.**

- 2026-09-07 · (METHOD, twice in one day) **An audit concluded absence from a view it had narrowed itself** · The M8.6 audit reported that the crew's spawn was missing from the book of record; it had grouped rows by `event` and `kind: 'spawn'` rows carry no `event` field, so 183 rows were invisible to the survey. The M8.4 audit then reported that `home/seeded-config` had never fired; it had read `Counter(...).most_common(12)` and the row, with count 1, sat outside the truncation. Both claims were written into PROGRESS and both had to be corrected there. **A filter, a truncation and a projection are all narrowings, and absence measured through one is not absence.** Before writing "there is no X": group by SHAPE rather than by the field you expected (`Counter(tuple(sorted(row.keys())))`), print the full distribution rather than its head, and state the population you searched. The degradation channel disproving one of these claims from its own rows is M8.2 working exactly as intended.

- 2026-09-07 · (M8.2 AUDIT) **The ladder and the dedupe hold, checked against the rows they produced** · `agora/log-size` appears at `count: 1` and then `count: 10` with nothing between — the power-of-ten ladder, in production — and `usage/pacing` shows a raise followed by its `cleared` row, which is the pair a condition-shaped kind exists to make possible. 59 degradation rows across 41 distinct causes. The dedupe's load-bearing property was re-checked by inventory rather than by test: every dynamic cause in `src/` embeds a BOUNDED identity — agent, adapter, instance, gate, task, trigger or check id, or a settings path — and none embeds a message, timestamp or free text. That is what stops a flood making every report unique and evicting the ring, and it is the property to re-check whenever a package adds a cause.

- 2026-09-07 · (M8.0 AUDIT) **Prove a tripwire with a probe, and check the exit code of the script rather than of `tail`** · Both halves of the seam rule were verified by planting rather than reading: a new `src/shared/audit-probe.ts` mapped to no subsystem and imported by nothing was refused with exit 1 and a sentence naming the file and its three remedies, and the coverage check refuses rather than passes when it cannot establish coverage. Neither allowlist has been gamed — `UNREACHABLE_ALLOWLIST` is untouched since the commit that created it, and the untested-module record has fallen from 24 to 22 with nothing from this session in it. The real evidence for this package is the other nine audits: in one day the gate caught two coverage dilutions in code written that same day, a module belonging to no subsystem, and a stale linux record. **A gate is load-bearing when it fails on your OWN work, and this one did, four times.**

- 2026-09-07 · (M8.0 AUDIT, CORRECTION) **`main` is protected; the register said it was not** · M8.0 closed with a standing Architect finding: "`main` has NO branch protection and PR #6 merged over a red code job, so every CI gate is advisory until required checks are enabled". Verified through the API rather than assumed: `main` now requires "Typecheck · lint · test", "Docs integrity" and "Commit attribution", with `strict: true` and `enforce_admins: false`. The strictness is not theoretical — it refused a merge on 2026-09-06 until the branch was brought up to date with a base that had moved. The gates are REQUIRED, not advisory, and the note is corrected in place. **A standing finding is a claim with a date on it; re-check the world before repeating one, especially when it says a control is absent.**

- 2026-09-07 · (AUDIT, and the answer was no) **The drive did not crash, and saying so took six read-only checks** · The Architect reported a drive crash after a session of heavy test runs and asked what caused it. Nothing did. `Get-PhysicalDisk` reports the NVMe SSD `Healthy`/`OK`, `Get-Volume C:` reports `Healthy`, the System log holds **zero** disk, Ntfs, storahci, stornvme or volmgr errors across three days, there is no Event 41 (dirty shutdown) and no Event 1001 (bugcheck), and the 05:47 restart is `Kernel-Power 109/577` — *system initiated reboot* — bracketed by a clean EventLog 6006→6005 pair, with NTFS Event 98 declaring every volume healthy on the way back up. It was a Windows-initiated reboot 2h40m after the last heavy run. Nothing had left the machine either: no branch on `origin`, no PR, and **zero files modified in the live `~/.ephesus`** — the one test that read the Architect's real `log.jsonl` only read it. **The useful part of the audit was not the answer but the two defects it found on the way**, neither of which threatened the drive and both of which had been invisible for two weeks: a suite that cannot say it is out of memory, and 3 279 leaked temp directories. Record the negative result as carefully as a positive one — an audit that finds nothing wrong with what it was pointed at, and two real things beside it, is the ordinary shape of a good audit.

- 2026-09-07 · (METHOD, a hypothesis killed by its own measurement) **The suite was not what ate the machine, and the first draft of the fix was therefore wrong** · Free memory had fallen to 0.11 GB of 16.8 while the suite ran, and the obvious inference — the suite exhausted the machine — was written up and half-implemented as a worker-count cap before it was measured. Sampling `Get-Process node` every 8 s through a full run: **peak 31 worker processes holding 1 986 MB in total**, largest single process 162 MB, free memory never below 1.43 GB. The suite wants about 2 GB, not 14. Something else held ~13 GB and the suite then could not get its share. So the cap would have fixed the wrong thing and slowed every run for no benefit, and it was deleted rather than shipped. **The measurement that refutes your fix is worth more than the one that confirms it, and it costs one run.** What the evidence DID support was a floor: refuse below 1 GB, which sits below every run that has worked (1.4 GB+) and above every run that has died (0.11–0.37 GB).

- 2026-09-07 · (METHOD) **The guard written to catch the defect survived being fed the defect** · A test file had called `mkdtempSync` per test and removed nothing — 1 391 of 3 279 leaked directories, the largest single source. One missing line, invisible in review, so the fix was a guard over the tree rather than an edit. Written as `source.includes('removeTempDir')`, it was then fed the real defect as a mutation: delete the teardown's body. **It survived** — deleting the body leaves the `import { removeTempDir }` line, so the file still *mentioned* the helper while removing nothing. The guard now checks for a **call**, and that mutant is kept as its regression. This is the repository's oldest recurring defect, a shape check standing in for a semantic one, reproducing inside the very test written to prevent it — which is the argument for feeding every new checker the case it must fail, not merely the cases it must pass.

- 2026-09-07 · (OPERATIONS, the multi-session hazard made concrete) **`main` moved seven commits under an in-flight branch, and the first sign was a coverage number that would not reproduce** · While M8.9 was being built in one worktree, a second session in the primary worktree advanced `main` from `956b434` to `6162e06` — seven commits including a `ProfilesPanel` fix with a new DOM test, a `log.ts` fix, a README-currency gate and a new CI job. Nothing announced it. It surfaced as a puzzle: `panels` branch coverage measured 39.91% on the M8.9 tree and 41.07% on a branch cut from `main` hours later, with no renderer change between them. Chasing that number to its cause — a per-file coverage diff naming `ProfilesPanel.tsx` at 35→64 lines — is what revealed the divergence. **Two consequences worth carrying:** a branch's base can go stale mid-session without any local signal, so `git log main..HEAD` and `git log <base>..main` belong in the pre-PR checklist; and a trial merge is cheap insurance — this one showed M8.9 conflicts only in the append-only DECISIONS-LOG, but **fails the README-currency gate `main` added while it was being written**, which is a red CI run avoided by ten minutes of checking.
- 2026-09-07 · (M8.9, B14 — THE DESIGN QUESTION, ANSWERED BEFORE ANYTHING WAS DRAWN) **The incident surface is DERIVED, and ADR-0027 had already said so** · The register set the condition that ADR-0027's test — *is this held, or derived?* — be answered before a line of panel was written, because `IncidentEndpoint` reaches the world only through `onLogEvent` and a surface must therefore either fold `log.jsonl` or gain a store. It is derived, and the ADR does not merely permit that: **§5's first bullet names incident correlation as deliberately NOT persisted**, on the grounds that a restart SHOULD re-raise a still-failing incident, and its closing line forbids the alternative by name — *do not persist state that a live subsystem re-derives from a durable source*. Every incident fact is already in the book of record, verbatim and append-only: raised, refused, declined, accepted, triaged with the agent's own summary, verification asked, verdict recorded, announcement owed. An `incidents.json` would be a second truth for facts that already have one, would silently reverse §5's recorded decision, and would go stale the moment a row was appended by anything but itself. So the panel is a fold: `foldIncidents(entries)` in `src/shared/incident-view.ts`, read through one new IPC channel over `Agora.readLogAll()`, with no new record and no new writer. **What the fold could not do until this package fixed it:** a refusal row carried no incident key, so twelve refusals on the Architect's machine could be counted and not attributed — which is why `incident-triage-refused` and `incident-verdict-refused` gained `incident` in `26d380b` before the surface was built rather than after. Rejected: a store keyed by incident (reverses ADR-0027 §5); folding in the renderer (the renderer never reads files, invariant §2); a `raisedKeys()` IPC (in-memory, so it answers "since the last restart" while claiming to answer "what happened").

- 2026-09-07 · (M8.9, B14) **A refusal that names no incident gets its own row rather than being dropped** · The fold has a bucket for refusals it cannot attribute, and this is the whole point of the surface rather than an edge case. A parse failure is exactly the case where the incident key is unknowable — the body did not parse, so nothing in it can be trusted to name one — and `in_reply_to` resolves it only when the sender threaded the reply. Discarding those would mean the panel reported *fewer* refusals than happened, which is the failure mode the panel exists to end: 12 of 21 triage attempts refused with nothing anywhere to show it. Attaching them to a guessed incident would be worse than dropping them, because a refusal filed against the wrong incident is a fact nobody can correct. So they are listed, with the sender, the subject and the reasons, under a heading that says they could not be attributed. **A surface that under-reports is the absence it was built to replace.**

- 2026-09-07 · (M8.9, B14 — WHERE IT LIVES) **A panel inside PROFILES, not a fourteenth tab** · UI-DESIGN §4 enumerates the Command Center tabs and there is no Incidents or Harbor among them; adding one would be inventing UI (BUILD-PROMPT §7). The incident path is a profile instance's: the binding comes from an activation plan's `ci` trigger, every incident row is `kind: 'profile'`, and the panel's first question — *who is on call for this repository* — is already answered on that tab. `SecretsPanel` and `SettingsPanel` inside the Watch set the panel-within-a-tab precedent at M7.6 and 2026-09-06 respectively. Rejected: a new top-level tab (invents UI and separates incidents from the instances that own them); the Watch (it is the safety surface — gates, spend, ceilings — and an incident is neither); the Activity feed (it already carries these rows as raw log lines, which is exactly the reading the fold exists to replace).

- 2026-09-07 · (M8.9, THE REFUSAL-QUALITY CHOICE THE REGISTER LEFT OPEN) **An over-long verdict is REFUSED with the limit named, not truncated with a marker** · The register named both options and required the build session to pick one and record it. Refuse. Truncating rewrites a verifier's reasoning, and a `because` cut mid-qualification — *"the claim holds only if you ignore that the file was renamed, which"* — is a claim nobody made, which is the exact failure `checkVerdict` exists to catch one layer down. Refusal is the CHEAP option here because of a property of the code rather than a hope: `refuseVerdict` never clears `awaitingVerdict`, so the thread stays open and the corrected answer lands on it. That property is now a test (*accepts the shortened second answer on the same thread*) precisely because it is the whole argument, and an implementation detail nobody pinned would have been changed by the next person who touched the method. **Three parts, because the message alone would not have stopped the waste:** `prompts/harbor/incident-verify-body.md` now STATES the two limits (a limit an agent is never told is one it can only discover by losing a turn to it — and the verifier had never been told), `parse-reasons.ts` re-words the size issues against the value actually sent (`because: 4231 characters, and the limit is 2000`, because the cap alone does not say whether to cut fifty characters or four thousand), and a refusal that leaves the thread open says so — deliberately NOT on the three paths where nothing is awaiting an answer, since telling an agent to answer again a question nobody asked buys a second wasted turn instead of saving one.

- 2026-09-07 · (M8.9, B15) **A deadline, not a catch: the two ways main dies do not look alike** · The one-shot bridge probe was the reported defect, and re-running it would have fixed only half. A main process that THREW rejects the invoke and the existing `.catch()` has always handled that; a main process whose event loop is BLOCKED never settles the invoke at all, so a catch-based heartbeat would have sat silent through exactly the failure it was written for. What distinguishes hung from idle is the answer not ARRIVING, which is a fact about the clock — hence `stallOf` and a deadline of three poll periods. One period would flag every ordinary scheduling hiccup and teach the Architect to ignore the warning, which is worse than not showing it. The strip also had to gain its own clock: a stall is the ABSENCE of events, so a component that only re-renders on one cannot show a silence growing. `watchingSince` is not an edge case but the load-bearing half — it is what ages a poll that has NEVER answered, so a main process that was already dead at mount is reported instead of reading "connecting…" for as long as the window is open. And a backwards clock reports no stall with no guard for it, because a negative silence is already less than any deadline; a second branch there would be one no mutation could kill.

- 2026-09-07 · (M8.9, METHOD) **Attribute a coverage drop by running the gate on `main`, not by reasoning about it** · The seam rule failed `panels` branches at 39.91% against a win32 floor of 40.26% on the very first full run of this package. Nothing in the diff touched a panel, and the reasoning-from-the-diff answer ("my change cannot have caused it") was almost certainly right — which is exactly why it is not evidence. Checking out `main` at `956b434` and running the same gate produced the IDENTICAL 39.91%: the floor was recorded at `ca1158a`, before `SettingsPanel.tsx` (290 lines) merged, so the record was stale for `main` itself and every session after that merge would have met the same red. Cost: one 165-second run. **A number is attributed by reproducing it without your change, not by arguing that your change could not have produced it** — and the package then FIXED it, because covering `App.tsx` for the first time lifted panels lines 34.5 -> 42.43.

- 2026-09-07 · (M8.9, METHOD, a measurement that did not happen) **Do not run anything else during a corroborating run** · The win32 ratchet needs three runs of one tree. Runs 2 and 3 were launched in the background so documentation could be written alongside them, and both produced no coverage report at all — the concurrent work starved vitest's forks, which died mid-run ("Worker exited unexpectedly"), and a `grep` filter on the output hid the failure until the ratchet came back at 1 of 3. Re-run undisturbed, run 2 recorded normally. This is the same rule the drafts work recorded as *never mutate during a measurement run*, one step out: **a measurement run owns the machine, and a filter that only greps for the good line cannot tell a crash from a silence.** Runs 2 and 3 then failed for a different reason entirely: free memory fell to **0.11 GB of 16.8 GB**, vitest's forks started dying, and the suite reported first 15 and then 39 "failures" that were nothing of the kind. Two observations settled that without a code hunt — the same tree ran green at `--maxWorkers=4` with byte-identical totals (8174/10625 lines), and the ordinary command succeeded the moment free memory returned to 2.78 GB. **Read `os.freemem()` before believing a suite that starts failing in batches**, and note what the identical totals also buy: worker count demonstrably does not move this measurement.

- 2026-09-07 · (M8.10, D3 — THE DESIGN QUESTION, ANSWERED BEFORE ANYTHING WAS WRITTEN) **Rotation moves bytes between files and changes nothing about what is readable** · The register asked for rotation and named the hazard itself: `incident-view.ts` drops an incident whose `raised` row is missing, so a rotation that put that row in an archive nobody read would show an EMPTY incident panel — not a stale one — with the incidents still open and nothing in the company saying they had ever existed. The readers were therefore enumerated first, and the answer was the same for all six: the incident board, the standup, the org metrics, the Gymnasium history, the degradation replay and the Activity feed all need the whole history. So none of them changed. The read path spans sealed segments and the live file, `readLogAll()` returns exactly what it returned before rotation existed, and rotation is invisible above the `Agora` seam. **The win is not "read less history"** — it is a bounded live file (git stops storing a whole new multi-megabyte blob per commit, because a sealed segment is one blob forever) and segment skipping decided from the FILENAMES with no I/O, since each segment is named for the first `seq` it holds. Measured on a 28.0 MB / 29 196-entry history: `tailLog(200)` 200 ms → 26 ms, `readLogSince(recent)` 150 ms → 21 ms, `readLogAll()` 171 ms → 178 ms. That last number is the honest cost of the decision and is reported rather than hidden: the whole-history read now opens eight files instead of one, and it is precisely the read that must not get cheaper by seeing less.
- 2026-09-07 · (M8.10, D3) **Append-only across a rotation boundary means the same LINES, not the same entries** · Rotation is a `rename` and nothing else — no rewrite, no compaction, no truncation, not one byte altered — so the concatenation of the segments and the live file IS the file that used to be there, in the same order.
