That is asserted the strong way: a test reads every line off disk, in file order, and checks the seq sequence is `1..n` with nothing missing and nothing duplicated. Asserting on the entries the READER returns would have been circular, since the reader is the thing under test. Two consequences worth naming: a torn line from a killed harness is sealed into a segment exactly as it stands (it was never readable and still is not, so nothing is lost), and `open()` must recover the sequence from the ARCHIVE when the live file is empty or absent — a restart landing just after a rotation would otherwise renumber from 1, colliding with archived entries and rewinding every cursor-based reader to the beginning of time.
- 2026-09-07 · (M8.10, D4 — THE RETENTION RULE, AND WHY A TRIM CANNOT LOSE MONEY) **Keep the newest eight session ids, drop from the front** · The register framed this correctly as a correctness question about spend rather than a performance one: a trimmed id is a transcript the budget can no longer read. It is safe for two reasons that have to hold TOGETHER. (1) Folded spend is durable — `CostLedger.fold` keeps a per-(agent, source) cursor and `spendFor` totals rows read back out of the store (invariant §11) — so dropping an id forgets where to look for MORE, not what was already found. (2) Only the newest session can still grow, because resume targets `sessionIds.at(-1)` and an engine writes the session it is running; so trimming from the FRONT can only ever drop transcripts that are finished. Two would satisfy that argument (the live session, plus one for an engine that forks on resume); eight is that minimum with a wide margin for engines that interleave sessions inside one spawn, because being wrong in that direction costs a lost fold and being generous costs seven file reads. **The owed test asserts the SPEND, not the rule**: twelve sessions folded, four ids trimmed, every token still counted.
- 2026-09-07 · (M8.10, D10) **A value resolved once at hire, because the two roster write sites do not have the same view** · `profile` was hard-coded null at both sites, so an activated crew was recorded as a floor of standalone hires. The fix is not "ask the resolver at both sites": `onRosterChange` REPLACES the entry, and the second site runs on lifecycle changes including the exit — by which time the instance may have been released, so a fresh lookup would answer null and erase what the hire recorded. That is the same erasure the `budget` field on the very next line is already commented to avoid. So it is resolved once, while the activation plan is still in flight and `planFor` can answer, and carried on the live agent; an agent does not change profile during its life, so one resolution is the whole truth and re-asking could only ever lose it. The value is the profile NAME, not the instance id: SDD §4.1 caps the field at 64 characters and documents it as the profile, while an instance id is `<profile>@<targetRef>` and carries a target the field does not claim to hold.
- 2026-09-07 · (M8.10, D5 — VERIFIED OPEN BY EXECUTION BEFORE ANYTHING WAS BUILT) **`isIdle` cannot tell a busy agent from a gone one, and the watchdog heard both as one silence** · The register was written before `f6c9262` and the merged `fix/mail-lost-when-a-woken-agent-dies`, either of which might have closed this, so it was probed rather than assumed: a message delivered into a dead agent's inbox, sitting there across three wake ticks, nobody nudged, and exactly one row in the book of record — `delivery`. Those two fixes address the agent that COMES BACK; nothing addressed the one that does not. The missing question is "is there a process at all", now `hasSession`, checked BEFORE the idle test. **The mail is not bounced and not dropped**: an agent can come back, the respawn ladders exist for that, and its inbox is where its mail belongs until it does — bouncing would break the one case the ladder was built for. What was missing is not delivery, it is DISCLOSURE (invariant §7). Reported once per stranded SET rather than per tick, because the sweep runs every second and a condition that re-reports every second is a condition nobody reads; holding the set rather than a flag is what lets new mail for a still-silent agent be heard.
- 2026-09-07 · (M8.10, METHOD — a survivor that was a missing test, not an equivalent mutant) **Two of twenty-eight mutations survived, and both named a case no test had thought of** · Read before either test was touched, as M8.0's rule requires. The first widened a cursor skip from `afterSeq + 1` to `afterSeq + 2`, which drops a segment whose LAST entry is the very next one the caller asked for; it survived because every cursor in the suite sat in the middle of a segment, and the standup's cursor is wherever the last brief ended — an arbitrary seq that lands on a boundary as readily as anywhere else. The replacement asserts, around EVERY boundary, that a cursor read equals filtering the whole history. The second deleted the refusal to seal onto an existing segment; `renameSync` replaces its destination silently, so that is a sealed segment destroyed. It survived because the harness cannot reach that state on its own — `seq` only rises — and the honest conclusion was NOT that the guard is equivalent: the state arrives from outside (a restore, a file-sync client putting a segment back, the second process pointed at one home that this module's contract already takes seriously), so the test builds it directly, because how it arises is not what the guard is responsible for.
- 2026-09-07 · (M8.10, METHOD) **The third survivor was the one written to survive** · A no-op edit was included in the mutation set on purpose. A harness that reports every mutation killed is a harness that cannot tell you when it has stopped running your tests, and the cheapest way to know it is honest is to hand it something it MUST fail to kill. It survived; the other twenty-seven did not.
- 2026-09-07 · (M8.10, METHOD, and it cost nothing because the gate caught it) **The seam rule found an untested new function before any human read the diff** · `check-coverage.cjs` failed the `company` subsystem on branches and functions immediately after D10 landed. The temptation is to attribute a coverage drop to noise or to a neighbouring package; the cheap hypothesis was tested first instead — a new public method, `ProfileActivations.profileFor`, with no test, because the D10 tests INJECT that seam into `AgentManager` and therefore never exercise the real resolver. Testing it cleared both floors on the next run, which settles the attribution by execution rather than by argument. **The lesson is the one M8.6 already recorded in another form:** a rule tested at the seam is not tested at the place that answers it, and the timing was the part that could have been wrong — the roster entry is written DURING the spawn, before the instance is registered, so a resolver that only consulted live instances would answer null for every hire in the crew it is naming.
- 2026-09-07 · (M8.10, TOOLING) **A suite nobody runs defends nothing: the rotation threshold is injectable, and the shipped value is pinned by its own test** · The first draft of the rotation suite rotated at the shipped 4 MiB and took **99 seconds**, because every boundary cost four megabytes of writes. `rotateAtBytes` is now a constructor option — the same shape the Agora already uses for `slowReadMs` — and the tests rotate at 64 KiB, which brings the file to 6.5 seconds. Everything about the mechanism (the rename, the naming, the skipping, the seq recovery) is a function of the boundary and not of where the boundary sits. Two cases guard the convenience from becoming the shipped value: one asserts `ROTATE_AT_BYTES` is 4 MiB, and one builds an `EventLog` with NO options and proves it has not rotated well past the test threshold.
- 2026-09-07 · (M8.10, AND THE ANSWER WAS "GIT IS BEHAVING NORMALLY") **2 547 loose objects with no packs is not a missing `gc`, it is a threshold not yet reached** · The register listed the Agora's git state alongside the log-size problem, which invites adding a `gc` call. Checked instead of assumed: the Agora commits through porcelain `git commit`, which already runs `gc --auto`, and `gc.auto` is unset — so the default of **6 700** loose objects governs, and 2 547 is simply below it. Nothing is missing and no call was added. What was actually wrong is the SIZE of those objects: a 28 MB `log.jsonl` re-committed produces a whole new multi-megabyte blob every time. Rotation fixes that at its source, because a sealed segment is written once and never changes again.
- 2026-09-07 · (RECORD REPAIR) **The entry warning about control characters had been written with control characters in it** · `docs/DECISIONS-LOG.md` carried a literal NUL at byte 379 988 and a literal backspace at 380 068 — inside the 2026-09-07 entry that warns about exactly this. The consequence was not cosmetic: `grep` classified the whole file as binary, so `grep "2026-09-07" docs/DECISIONS-LOG.md` printed *"Binary file matches"* and no lines at all, and the book of decisions could not be searched by the ordinary tool. Repaired byte-precisely to the escapes that were meant (`\u0000`, `\b`), so nothing else in the record moved; the file now returns its 21 matching lines.
- 2026-09-07 · (M8.11, AUDIT BEFORE BUILD — the habit M8.9 and M8.10 both paid for) **Two of the register's M8.11 items were already closed, and one was not** · The package line asks for the conformance table to "gain an autonomy case". It has one: `describe('autonomy grade honesty (ADR-0031)')` at `test/conformance/adapter-conformance.ts:280`, running over all four subjects, landed with ADR-0031 on 2026-09-06. `assertAutonomyEnforceable` likewise exists and is called from `AgentManager.spawn`. Both verified by execution before anything was written, and neither was rebuilt. What was NOT there: `grep -rn "REFERENCE_ENGINE\|non-reference" src/` returned nothing — there was no refusal path of any kind, anywhere, for any engine. The audit cost ten minutes and would have cost an hour of duplicate work.
- 2026-09-07 · (M8.11, D1 — WHERE THE REFUSAL LIVES, and it is not the registry) **`activationPlan`, beside the refusals that are already there** · ADR-0024 §1 says "refused at profile load", and there were two candidates. `EngineRegistry.get` was rejected: its question is *does this build carry an adapter*, and folding *may a hire run on it* into the same lookup would make the conformance suite — which constructs `CodexAdapter` and `GeminiAdapter` DIRECTLY, on purpose — start failing or start special-casing Claude, which is the one reading ADR-0024's "What this decision is NOT" forbids by name. `activationPlan` was chosen because it is both the activation preview and the plan activation executes (its own contract says so), so the screen and the outcome cannot disagree about a refusal; it is pure, so the refusal is table-testable; and it runs BEFORE agent ids are claimed, worktrees are cut or trust records are written, so a refusal is a refusal rather than a half-activated company to unwind. `AgentManager.spawn` still refuses an unregistered id as the backstop for spawn paths that never came through a profile (the `agents:spawn` IPC).
- 2026-09-07 · (M8.11, D2 — THE RENAME IS A SCHEMA MIGRATION, and the machine it was written on would not have noticed) **Accept the retired grade on READ; `schemaVersion` stays 1** · `hookFidelity` is validated on every entry of `agora/registry.json` by `hookSupportSchema`, and `Agora.registry()` falls back to an EMPTY roster on a parse failure and then refuses to overwrite the file it could not read. So dropping `pty-heuristic` from the enum without a migration would, at the first boot after an upgrade, cost a company every seat on its roster and say so in one warning line. Bumping `REGISTRY_SCHEMA_VERSION` to 2 was rejected because it makes that failure *worse*, not better: `z.literal(2)` refuses every roster version 1 ever wrote, which is all of them. The chosen shape is a separate `storedHookSupportSchema` — a `z.preprocess` over `LEGACY_HOOK_SUPPORTS` — used ONLY where a durable file is read. Deliberately not the same schema the code writes with: one schema doing both would put `pty-heuristic` back into the `HookSupport` type and hand every consumer a fourth case forever. It terminates without a rewrite-in-place, because the value the roster is next written from is the normalized one. The Architect's live registry today reads `{"native": 7}`, so nothing here would have broken on this machine — which is luck, and the tests run against a real `Agora` and a real roster file carrying the retired string instead.
- 2026-09-07 · (M8.11, D3 — ASKED RATHER THAN INHERITED) **`ENGINE_IDS` keeps `grok` and `opencode`** · The objection is fair: a hire schema that accepts a string nothing can ever spawn is the same class of lie as the README, and neither has ever had an adapter. But the lie is that the string is *accepted silently*, and the refusal closes it — a `grok` hire is now refused by the same sentence, with the same reason, as a `codex` one, and that is asserted for all five non-reference ids. Narrowing the roster was rejected on three counts: `EngineId`'s membership is written into ADR-0009's own interface, so removing two would be an ADR edit rather than an implementation; ADR-0024 is "deliberately reversible" and deleting the vocabulary makes reversing it larger; and the conformance table asserts every adapter's id is in `ENGINE_IDS`, which is a check that only means something while the roster is the seam's roster rather than the shipping list.
- 2026-09-07 · (M8.11, D4 — THE WATCH PANEL'S SENTENCE WAS WRONG IN BOTH DIRECTIONS) **`protectionFor` loses its `blind` list rather than growing it** · It answered `blind: ['repetition', 'error-rate']` and the panel printed it. ADR-0024 names one error — it implies burn-rate still protects such an engine, when burn-rate fires on `budgetState === 'breached'` and a budget breaches on ledger rows compiled from a TRANSCRIPT that an adapter with no transcript reader never produces. The other error is the opposite: `hop-cap` counts Hermes escalations and owes nothing to hooks, so it is NOT lost, and listing it would have been a new wrong sentence in place of the old one. The blind set is therefore not a function of the hook grade at all — it is a function of two independent adapter properties — and a lookup keyed on the grade cannot answer it honestly. The grade answers the one question it can: is this engine's breaker weaker. `BreakerState.blindSignals` is deleted with it, so the wrong sentence is not one `.join()` away from the panel again.
- 2026-09-07 · (M8.11, D5 — NOT BUILT, AND THAT IS THE DECISION) **`AVATAR_STATES` gains no `unknown`** · ADR-0024's Context lists "the floor asserts a confident `idle` forever" as one of five symptoms, and its Decision has four items, none of which is a new avatar state. That is consistent rather than an oversight: the confident `idle` exists because a hook-less engine produces no events, and the refusal removes the hook-less engine rather than giving its silence a nicer pose. Adding `unknown` now would be building a disclosure for a case this package makes unreachable — and SDD §6 names ten states, so a new one is an SDD amendment, not an implementation detail. It becomes owed again the moment ADR-0024 is revisited.
- 2026-09-07 · (M8.11, D6 — THE RISK THIS PACKAGE CARRIES IS DELETION, so the guard is a roll-call) **The conformance table records which subjects ran, and a test asserts all four** · ADR-0024 keeps `codex.ts` and `gemini.ts` in the tree for one reason: they are the suite's second implementation, and "a suite with one implementation only proves that implementation compiles". The way that value is lost is not an argued deletion — it is a `runAdapterConformance(...)` call quietly going away with the registration it looked like it belonged to, after which the suite is green, shorter, and proves less. A count read off a scroll-back is not a check, so `CONFORMANCE_SUBJECTS` is asserted to be exactly `['fake engine', 'claude code', 'codex', 'gemini']`. Paired with `scripts/reachability.cjs`, which now fails if either adapter becomes reachable again *or* stays unreachable without the ADR-0024 entry, the two halves of §4 are both mechanical.
- 2026-09-07 · (M8.11, ENVIRONMENT — recorded because a gate that fails for a non-code reason is a gate somebody will learn to ignore) **`npm run test:coverage` exits non-zero on an EPERM that is not a test failure, intermittently, on this machine** · Observed on 2 of 3 full runs of the identical tree: every test passes (`219 files, 4136 passed, 8 skipped, 0 failed`), the coverage report is written, and vitest THEN fails tearing down its own scratch directory — `EPERM: operation not permitted, rmdir '…/coverage/.tmp'`. That non-zero exit short-circuits the `&&` chain in the Definition-of-Done line, so `check-coverage`, `check-readme-current` and `check-attribution` never run, and the transcript reads as a failed gate. **It is a handle race, not a permission fault**: `coverage/.tmp` is empty and removable moments later, and the worktree lives under a OneDrive-synced path where a sync or scanner handle outliving the process is the known failure family (see the git-worktree-delete note). The specific holder was NOT isolated — OneDrive and Defender are equally plausible and neither was proved — so this records the SHAPE and its condition, not a cause. **Nothing was changed to work around it.** Weakening the gate to tolerate a non-zero `test:coverage` would make it unable to report a real suite failure, which is the trade this repository refuses; the three checks were run separately and passed. Whether the gate should distinguish "the suite failed" from "the runner could not tidy up" is a process change and therefore a Gymnasium proposal (ADR-0015), not something to fix in a work package — flagged to the Architect in the M8.11 session report.

Only DD-6 is open as written.
- 2026-09-07 · (M8 LIVE, ARCHITECT DECISION, EXECUTED) **The Architect's own gate policy predated the decision that set it, and was looser in three places** · `home.ts` seeds `gate-policy.json` only when the file is ABSENT — deliberately, because `~/.ephesus` is the Architect's copy — so a machine configured before a decision never receives it. Consequence to expect: the next launch WILL hire Artemis, because what was refusing her is gone.

A third, opposite case the same day: Artemis's boot refusal prints nothing to stdout, which looked like an invariant §7 violation until `artemis.start`'s catch was read — it calls `reportDown`, which writes the `orchestrator/down` row. The pattern in all three is the same: absence in one vocabulary is not absence.
- 2026-09-07 · (M8.12, D1 — THE DECISION THE PACKAGE TURNS ON, and refusing to leave it implicit is half of it) **Consent covers the company STARTING WORK, not just the hire** · "The gate guards the hire" and "the gate guards the company starting work" are different products. A gate on `artemis.start` alone leaves `scheduler.start()` forty lines above it, and `~/.ephesus/triggers.json` shows `standup`, `retro` and `gym-metric-check` sharing the timestamp `1788631795998` — so the first tick fires all three together, sixty seconds after a boot nobody authorised. Both are therefore withheld until consent is on file, and the live run measured both halves: 227 seconds and four scheduler ticks after a boot on the Architect's own machine with no consent record, zero `spawn` rows and zero cadence rows were written; a consented boot on a scratch home wrote `orchestrator/consented` at t+0 and `triggers.json` with all four cadences at t+60s. The clock is withheld WHOLE rather than per-trigger even though `Scheduler.enabled()` already exists and would have been the smaller diff: a trigger armed later by a restored activation (ADR-0027) would be armed *unguarded*, and nothing would report the day that started being true. Recorded as [ADR-0032](adr/ADR-0032-the-company-asks-before-it-starts.md).
- 2026-09-07 · (M8.12, D2 — ABSENT MEANS ASK, and this is the M8.4 trap restated) **A missing consent record reads as "ask", never as "assume yes" and never as a durable "no"** · `ensureHarnessHome` seeds a file only when it is ABSENT, deliberately, so a decision never reaches a machine set up before it — which is exactly how the Architect's `gate-policy.json` sat three days behind DD-1. Every install that exists today has no consent record, so `undefined` reading as granted would make the gate a no-op on every machine anybody actually has, and reading as a durable refusal would be a "no" nobody could lift. Only a grant is written; a decline is the absence of one, because the withheld state IS the question. A corrupt `config.json` also reads as "ask", and that falls out of the existing design rather than being added: `configSchema` is strict, so a malformed block invalidates the file, `home.ts` runs on `defaultConfig`, and `defaultConfig` carries no consent. Tested against a pre-M8.12 `config.json` written to disk and read through the real `ensureHarnessHome`, not only against a fresh home.
- 2026-09-07 · (M8.12, D3 — WHERE THE RECORD LIVES) **Consent is a field of `config.json`, not a file of its own** · Invariant §9 asks for a `schemaVersion` and a validator in `src/shared/`; `config.json` has both, and `mode` and `everEnabledImproving` are already exactly this kind of state. A second durable file would be a second thing to seed, migrate and keep honest for one record. The field is optional, so every existing `config.json` stays valid with no migration. The record is `{ grantedAt, terms }`: `terms` is the `CONSENT_TERMS_VERSION` the grant was made against, so widening what consent covers can ask again instead of silently carrying a narrower agreement onto new behaviour; a grant against a NEWER version than this build asks about is honoured, because re-asking somebody to consent to a subset of what they already allowed is an interrogation rather than a safeguard. `grantedAt` is never rewritten by a later grant.
- 2026-09-07 · (M8.12, D4 — WHERE THE DECISION LIVES, and it is not style) **`decideConsent` is a pure function in `src/shared/`; `CompanyStart` owns the ordering in `src/main/`; `index.ts` keeps the construction and one `boot()` call** · `index.ts` boot wiring has produced three dead-code findings in this repository — the Herald, M7.2's inert trigger, M7.7's silent standup — every one behind a green suite, because nothing short of a real Electron boot could reach the code. A refusal that could not be tested *in the direction of refusing* would be the worst possible member of that set. Production call path, stated per ENGINEERING-STANDARDS §6.7: `src/main/index.ts:3328` `companyStart.boot()`, constructed at `:2961` with `hire: hireOrchestrator` (`:2872`) and `startSchedule: () => scheduler.start()` (`:2968`); the grant path is `src/main/ipc.ts:611` → `deps.consent.grant()` → the same `startWork()`, reached from `src/preload/index.ts:74` and `src/renderer/src/ConsentGate.tsx`'s one button.
- 2026-09-07 · (M8.12, F1 DECIDED — readers tolerate it, and the CAUSE was in the writer all along) **`EventLog.read` treats its cursor as a POSITION in the file rather than a numeric threshold** · The choice was "tolerate explicitly" against "accept as a one-off scar", and what settled it is who loses the row: `BriefingJob.gather(sinceSeq)` pages from a cursor, so a hidden row is a hidden event in the standup — and SRS §6.1 asks for "the next briefing narrates the incident accurately from the log". The exit criterion's own evidence path is what was at stake. The cause is not in the data: `EventLog.seq` is an in-memory counter recovered at `open()`, so two harness processes on one home each recover the same high-water mark and both stamp `highest + 1`; the class comment claimed `O_APPEND` "covers the case of a second harness process", which is true of the bytes and false of the counter, and it now says so. **Two residuals, taken deliberately and pinned by tests**: a cursor sitting on the row immediately BEFORE the duplicate still misses it (one cursor value, down from three — closing it needs three interacting conditions in the reader every consumer shares, and a continuously-paging consumer never holds that cursor); and `sourcesFrom` skips whole segments by filename, so a duplicate inside an already-passed segment is still lost, which is the cost rotation exists to avoid. On a strictly increasing log the old and new rules agree line for line.
- 2026-09-07 · (M8.12, F2 PARTIALLY DECIDED — the documentation half is done, the design half is a must-ask) **The README now says to stop Electron by process, and whether the harness should REFUSE to boot on a home another instance holds goes to the Architect** · Stopping `npm run dev` leaves Electron alive; a second `npm run dev` then runs a second harness against the same `~/.ephesus`, which is where the duplicate `seq` above came from. That is a setup fact and belongs in the README, so it is there, in both PowerShell and POSIX form, in the setup section and in `docs/EXIT-M8.md` §4. A boot-time lock is a different thing: it touches ADR-0004's single-committer rule, needs a stale-lock policy and a refusal message, and would be the first thing that can stop the app from starting. Not built on this package's own initiative — raised as a §8.3 must-ask in the M8.12 session report with options and a recommendation.
- 2026-09-07 · (M8.12, D5 — WHERE THE EXIT SCRIPT LIVES) **`docs/EXIT-M8.md`, its own file, not an extension of TEST-STRATEGY** · TEST-STRATEGY says what tests are owed and which level covers what; this is an operator runbook for one human executing one acceptance criterion by hand, complete with what to break, when to restart and where each clause's evidence lands. Folding it in would make TEST-STRATEGY answer two questions with one voice. Linked from TEST-STRATEGY, PROGRESS and the README instead.
- 2026-09-08 · (M8.13, D1 — THE IDEA THE PACKAGE TURNS ON) **`DIAGNOSIS.md` reports three states, and the third is `NOT EXERCISED`** · A report saying "incidents: ok" because no incident has ever been raised would be exactly the check-that-cannot-fail this codebase keeps finding, dressed as a diagnostic. Two passes were already saved from it by a human writing "NOT exercised" instead of "fine" — the 2026-09-07 live verification (whose §6.3 exists for this reason) and the M8 exit run, which reported six clauses as NOT EXERCISED rather than banking a vacuous pass. This makes the distinction mechanical: a live degradation naming the area means `broken` with its reason; otherwise a log row that PROVES the area did its job means `working`, citing the row; otherwise `not-exercised`, saying what WOULD exercise it. The report says in its own body that NOT EXERCISED is not a pass, and a mutation removing that sentence is killed.
- 2026-09-08 · (M8.13, D2 — DERIVED, NEVER STORED) **The report folds `AgoraHealth.runtime` and `log.jsonl`; it adds no durable state** · The audit found the material already exists: the M8.2 degradation ring already carries one row per CAUSE with `count`, `since` and `freshness: 'live' | 'carried'`, and already survives a restart; the book of record already holds every positive event. What did not exist was any artifact holding them together — the harness writes `config.json`, `gate-policy.json`, `authority.json`, `breaker-stops.json`, `github-app.json` and `triggers.json`, all inputs and state, and not one account of what happened. So this is a reading, not a second copy (the M8.9 B14 rule). Markdown rather than JSON for the same reason: `log.jsonl` is already the machine-readable ground truth and a parseable twin would owe a schema and a validator under invariant §9 for no gain.
- 2026-09-08 · (M8.13, D3 — NO CLI, AND THAT IS THE TRADE) **The app writes the report; there is no `scripts/diagnose.cjs`** · An offline CLI is the obvious shape and it was refused twice over: `scripts/*.cjs` cannot import `src/shared/*.ts`, so it would re-implement `parseLogLine` and the degradation vocabulary in plain JS — two things that must agree — and a TypeScript runner is a new dependency, which BUILD-PROMPT §10 makes a must-ask for what is a convenience. The cost is real and is stated rather than hidden: if the app never starts, there is no fresh report. It is paid for by writing OFTEN (boot, every minute, and once more in the quit path) and by putting the file's own age in its FIRST line, so a stale report cannot be read as a current one — which is the failure that actually matters, and the one no writer can prevent by guaranteeing freshness.
- 2026-09-08 · (M8.13, THREE DEFECTS THE FIRST LIVE REPORT EXPOSED — all mine, all invisible to a green suite) **`npm run dev` found in one boot what 4,231 tests did not** · (a) It rendered withheld consent as **BROKEN**. It is not broken; it is the designed state, and a BROKEN on every healthy first launch is how a reader learns to skip the column. Fixed with a fourth verdict, `waiting`, declared per probe as `waitingCauses` so the table stays the one place that says what a condition MEANS — and `consent/unwritable`, which shares the same source, still reads `broken`. (b) It said "no schedule is armed, by design" and then **listed five armed schedules**: `scheduler.armed()` reports REGISTERED triggers, and the clock that fires them is behind the consent gate (ADR-0032), so on a withheld company that list is a forecast rather than a state. The heading now says which. (c) Worst: the rewrite trigger was registered on the **company scheduler**, whose clock is also behind the consent gate — so on a machine stuck at that gate, the state a stranger is most likely to be in, the report would have been written once at boot and never again. It now runs on its own `setInterval`, unref'd. A diagnostic that stops when its subject stops is not a diagnostic.
- 2026-09-08 · (M8.13, A SURVIVOR THAT WAS A MISSING TEST, not an equivalent mutant) **A LIVE condition must never appear in the carried section** · The mutation `carried = conditions.slice()` survived the first pass. The comfortable reading is "equivalent"; it is not. The carried section says its rows "were true when the harness stopped and nothing has re-checked them since" — of a condition that is true right now, that is a lie, and it is the exact confusion the two sections exist to prevent. The existing test asserted the forward direction (a carried condition is not live) and never the reverse. Test added, mutant re-run, killed. Third consecutive package where a survivor was read rather than patched around, and third where it turned out to be a gap.
- 2026-09-08 · (M8.13, THE COVERAGE FLOOR CAUGHT A DESIGN SMELL, not a testing gap) **`DiagnosisWriter` has no clock** · `agora` dropped below its branches and functions floors, and the cheap hypothesis was right: `src/main/diagnosis.ts` was 2/4 functions because `at()` and its `now` default had **no caller in production**. The fix was deletion, not a test. The writer renders against the instant its own snapshot was taken, so a clock of its own would have been a second source of time that had to agree with the first — and the floor is what noticed, which is the seam rule doing exactly its job (ENGINEERING-STANDARDS §6.7).
- 2026-09-08 · (M8.13, THE FIVE DEFECTS THE EXIT RUN FOUND — four in docs I had merged hours earlier) **The README and `EXIT-M8.md` sent a stranger to two panels that do not exist** · `IncidentsPanel` renders inside `ProfilesPanel`, deliberately — UI-DESIGN §4's tab list has no Incidents and the code says "do not invent UI" — and there is NO Harbor surface in the renderer at all, zero references. I wrote "the **INCIDENTS** tab" into both files and put "HARBOR: your open pull requests and recent CI runs are listed" into the exit script's pre-flight checkpoint: a table telling somebody to verify the run is ready by looking at a panel that has never existed. The code was right and the prose invented UI. Also fixed: "Node 20" against a lockfile where fifteen packages want `^20.19.0 || >=22.12.0` and `@electron/rebuild` wants `>=22.12.0` on the postinstall path, with no `engines` field to catch it; "Nothing else" falsified on first boot by two MemPalace degradations; and `EPH_HOME`, which was documented nowhere while `EXIT-M8.md` demanded a clean home and never said how to get one.
- 2026-09-08 · (M8.13, TWO MORE DEFECTS FROM PROVING IT — five in total, all found by running it rather than by testing it) **A designed state rendered as a fault, twice; and one question read from two sources** · (d) The FIRST report of a consented company said `consent: granted` in its header and `consent | NOT EXERCISED` in its table, because the header used the direct fact and the row scanned the log for `orchestrator/consented` — which the boot had not appended yet. Two readings of one question from two places is one bug waiting for a timing difference. Fixed by giving a probe an optional `provenDirectly`, so consent is settled by the fact the input already carries; the boot write also moved after the verdict reaches the log. (e) `spend` read `BROKEN` on a stock install, because `budgets/state:<agent>` fires whenever the state is not `ok` and `unbudgeted` is the SHIPPED DEFAULT (ADR-0029). Fixed by replacing the exact-match `waitingCauses` with a `waitingWhen(condition)` predicate — a breached budget arrives through the same cause and must still read `broken`, which an exact-match list could not express.
- 2026-09-08 · (M8.13, THE UNDERLYING GAP, flagged rather than fixed) **The degradation channel carries no SEVERITY, so every consumer has to guess** · Invariant §7 is right that every give-up must be visible, but it sends a deliberate default (`unbudgeted`), a state waiting on a human (`consent/not-granted`) and a genuine fault (`budgets/state` breached) down one pipe in one shape. `DIAGNOSIS.md` is the first consumer that has to tell them apart, and it does so by matching another module's wording — which is the "two things that must agree" smell, in a new place. The minimal honest thing was done (declarative, in the probe table, tested in both directions) and the real question — whether `DegradationRow` should carry a severity, which is a change to M8.2's shared schema and to every call site — is raised for the Architect rather than decided here.
- 2026-09-08 · (M8.13, AN OBSERVATION FROM PROVING IT, not a product defect) **A deep `EPH_HOME` breaks the Agora's git commits on Windows** · A restart proof against a home nested under the agent scratchpad failed with `error: open("agents/agent.artemis/inbox/.inflight/…json"): Filename too long` / `fatal: adding files failed`. That is MAX_PATH, caused by the test's own choice of a pathologically deep directory, and it disappeared at `%USERPROFILE%\ephproof`. Recorded because the README now tells people to set `EPH_HOME` and does not warn them: a home more than roughly 150 characters deep will make every Agora commit fail on Windows, and the failure surfaces as a git error rather than as advice. Worth a sentence in the README, and possibly a boot-time check — neither built here.
- 2026-09-08 · (ARCHITECT DECISION — WHAT ACTUALLY BLOCKS M8, and it is not the exit run) **Every control is buried in the renderer, and that is the defect** · The M8 exit run stalled because consent and profile activation have no path except the window, and a fresh agent could not click them. The instinct was to work around that — you click, or the agent gets desktop control. The Architect refused both and named the real problem: *"when it says architect activates the crew it can also mean for my verbal approvement. So we need to find a way for you to activate everything from CLI tools of Ephesus that we need to build. Thus all controls should not be buried under the UI of the application."* §6.1's "The Architect activates Skeleton Crew" is about AUTHORITY, not about a mouse. The unblock is therefore a control surface, built as **M8.14**, and the exit run follows it — so M8 closes on a run where a non-author performed every step with nobody clicking anything.
- 2026-09-08 · (ARCHITECT DECISION — the standard that lets the row close at all) **A fresh agent session SATISFIES §6.1's "a developer who is not the author"** · It has genuinely not seen the code and cannot remember which button to press, which is the property the clause exists to test. Recorded here rather than left as a judgement nobody can find later, because M7's exit has been open on this same clause since 2026-09-01 and "we never decided what counts" is why. One run now settles both milestones. What it is NOT: a licence for the author to run it and call it a proxy — the runner must have no memory of building the thing, and must declare which it is at the top of its record (`docs/EXIT-M8.md` §0).
- 2026-09-08 · (ARCHITECT DECISION — SRS §6.1 AMENDED) **The hour is Ephesus-side time, excluding CI latency** · Measured on the chosen target: `mertefesensoy/aftershock` takes 11–20 minutes per CI cycle and the Harbor ingests every 10 (`HARBOR_INGEST_EVERY_MS`), so detection alone can consume half the hour before the company has had a chance to do anything wrong. A wall-clock hour there mostly measures a GitHub queue nobody controls, and every downstream clause would read as failed when it was never reached. The criterion now asks whether the COMPANY responds within the hour, and the record must state which minutes were Actions latency and which were Ephesus. Amended on the record rather than quietly reinterpreted, which is the third of the three options BUILD-PROMPT already allows.
- 2026-09-08 · (ARCHITECT DECISION — §6.1's briefing clause) **The briefing may be convened by hand during the run, and the record must say so** · `STANDUP_EVERY_MS` is 24 hours, so "the next briefing narrates the incident accurately from the log" cannot fire inside the hour on shipped cadences. Shortening the cadence for the run was refused: that is changing the product to pass its own test. The clause is about whether the briefing NARRATES THE INCIDENT ACCURATELY — which is exactly what M7.7 found unreachable by construction — not about what time it runs. So convene one from the ODEON tab, and record plainly that it was convened rather than scheduled, so nobody later reads it as having fired on its own.
- 2026-09-08 · (M8.14 SCOPE — the control surface OPERATES, it does not AUTHORISE) **Consent, activation, briefings, status and reads; NOT gate approvals, memo verdicts, secrets or mode changes** · The excluded four are the decisions the Watch exists to put in front of a human. A CLI that could approve a destructive gate would delete the meaning of §6.1's own last clause — "with zero un-gated destructive actions" — by making the gate scriptable by the same automation the gate is there to bound. ADR-0010 (secrets write-only) and FR-14.2 (mode is the Architect's) already say the same thing in their own areas. The line is: a script may run the company; only a human may authorise what the company is not otherwise allowed to do.
- 2026-09-08 · (M8.14 TRUST — guarded like the event plane, and auditable beyond it) **Owner-only local endpoint, and every control act tagged `remote` in the book of record** · The transport mirrors `hookEndpointFor`: `<home>/events.sock`-style socket at 0600 on POSIX, and on Windows the LOCAL named-pipe namespace (libuv rejects remote clients) with a per-home sha256 discriminator so two `EPH_HOME`s cannot collide. No token: that would be a new secret to manage against ADR-0010's write-only rule, and the endpoint already trusts exactly what the hook endpoint, `~/.ephesus` and the engine credentials all trust — every process running as the Architect. What it adds beyond the hook endpoint is AUDIT: every act arriving over the control surface is tagged `remote` the way FR-10.3 tags inbound items, so the book of record always distinguishes what the window did from what a script did.
- 2026-09-08 · (M8.14, D1 — THE TAG IS A FIELD ON THE ACT'S OWN ROW, and the kind is the one FR-10.3 names) **`kind: 'remote'`, `event: 'control'`, `channel: 'remote'`** · Two shapes were possible and the difference matters. Stamping `channel: 'remote'` onto the act's OWN row — `orchestrator/consented` for a grant, `profile/activated` for an activation — keeps each act under the kind its consumers already read, but those rows are written by `CompanyStart` and `ProfileStore`, which do not know how they were reached; threading a channel down to them would put a provenance field on four modules for one caller's benefit. So the surface writes ONE audit row of its own, under the kind FR-10.3 already names, and the act's own row is untouched — `DIAGNOSIS.md`'s consent probe still finds `orchestrator:consented` exactly where it always did. `event: 'control'` distinguishes it from the Harbor's `remote` rows (`inbound`, `outbound-*`, `company-token-minted`), which are about the git remote rather than about a directive; the pair `remote:control` is what the new diagnosis probe greps for, in the CONSUMER's vocabulary.
- 2026-09-08 · (M8.14, D2 — READS ARE NOT WRITTEN DOWN, and that is a declared fact rather than an omission) **`writes: boolean` sits in the verb table beside the refusal list** · "Every control act is tagged `remote`" is the requirement, and the honest reading of *act* is *something that changed*. `log.jsonl` is append-only: a `status` polled once a second by a watching script would push real events out of a reader's view permanently, and no later decision can undo it. So the four verbs that change something are logged and the eight reads are not — plus every refusal, always, because an attempt to approve a gate from a script is exactly what an audit wants to find. Putting the flag in the same table as the refusal list is what makes it testable: `test/shared/control.test.ts` asserts the writing set by name, so a mutation that flips one is killed rather than merely noticed.
- 2026-09-08 · (M8.14, D3 — THE HARNESS ADVERTISES ITS ADDRESS; THE CLIENT DOES NOT COMPUTE IT) **`control-endpoint.json`, written atomically at boot and removed on a clean quit** · The obvious client re-derives `sha256(resolve(home))` in plain JavaScript, which is a second implementation of a rule that must agree with the first — the defect class this repository names by name, and the reason M8.13 refused an offline `scripts/diagnose.cjs`. The file also answers the tool's most common failure without a connection attempt: **no harness is running**, in English, naming the home. It carries `schemaVersion` and has its validator in `src/shared/control.ts` (invariant §9), and a stale one left by a crash produces a different sentence again — *"found an address, nothing is listening"* — because those are different problems with different fixes.
- 2026-09-08 · (M8.14, D4 — WHERE THE CLIENT LIVES, and what that costs) **`scripts/ephctl.cjs`, not `shims/`, and it is outside the coverage map** · `shims/` is what the harness hands an AGENT process through its spawn plan (`EPH_AGENT_ID`, `EPH_HOOK_TOKEN`, `EPH_HOOK_ENDPOINT`); this is what a person runs at the repo root, which is exactly how `docs/EXIT-M8.md` reaches it with no build step. The cost is stated rather than hidden: `productionFiles()` covers `src/**` and `shims/**/*.mjs`, so the coverage gate cannot see `scripts/`. Two things pay for it — `test/scripts/ephctl.test.ts` spawns the REAL file against a real `ControlServer` (the `eph-recall` pattern), and the client holds no verb table, no validators and no rendering, which a test asserts by reading its source. There is nothing in it to be wrong about except argv parsing and socket plumbing, and both are driven directly.
- 2026-09-08 · (M8.14, D5 — THE COVERAGE ROW, chosen for the standard it sets rather than for convenience) **Both new modules join `watch`** · `home` is 114 executable lines and `shims` 294: either would have jumped nine or ten points and failed as a STALE RECORD, and `boot` — the other candidate, since `src/main/ipc.ts` lives there — sits at 27.9% where a well-tested module would have lifted it past the five-point lag. `watch` is the honest row on its own terms: the surface's defining artifact is a boundary on AUTHORITY, `SourceChannel` already lives in `src/shared/gates.ts` in that row, and its floor (96.17 lines / 90.2 functions / 87.1 branches) is the highest standard in the map — which is the right bar for a new trust boundary, and it is what forced the branch work that a happier row would have let through.
- 2026-09-08 · (M8.14, A MECHANICAL CHOICE, recorded per BUILD-PROMPT §8.2) **`harnessHomeRoot()` treats `EPH_HOME=""` as unset** · Extracted from `initHome()` so `test/scripts/ephctl.test.ts` can pin the CLI's default against the app's rather than trusting a comment. The old expression was `process.env['EPH_HOME'] ?? path.join(os.homedir(), '.ephesus')`, which on an empty string resolved the home to `''` and failed inside `ensureHarnessHome`; the extracted version falls back, which is what every caller meant and what the CLI does.
- 2026-09-08 · (M8.14, A GAP FOUND WHILE INDEXING) **ADR-0032 was never listed in `docs/adr/README.md`** · Added, with ADR-0033. Noted because the index is how somebody finds an ADR they do not already know the number of, and an accepted decision missing from it is invisible to exactly the reader it was written for.
- 2026-09-08 · (M8.14, THE DEFECT THE LIVE RUN EXPOSED — known, worked around, and still open) **A grant given in THIS session wrote no row at all** · Only `boot()` logged `orchestrator/consented`. A company started from the banner — or from `ephctl` — produced four spawns with nothing above them saying why, and the row did not appear until the next restart. M8.13 had already met this and worked *around* it: its consent probe reads `provenDirectly` from `config.json` precisely because the log row was not there yet, which is a fix for the report and not for the book of record. The emission moved INTO `CompanyStart`, where the ordering already lives, so both callers get it and neither can forget; `boot()` writes `awaiting-consent` or `consented`, `grant()` writes `consented` through the same `startWork`, and the row carries `from: 'boot' | 'grant'` because "the company came up already consented" and "somebody said go at 03:14" are different afternoons to a reader asking why four agents spawned. `index.ts` lost a statement rather than gaining one, which is the direction the boot coverage row exists to keep true.
- 2026-09-08 · (M8.14, THE SECOND LIVE FINDING, small and exactly the kind the exit run trips on) **`profile:activate`'s usage line advertised `--isolation worktree`** · There is no such value: `activationIsolationSchema` accepts `as-declared`, `isolate-all` and `none`. A help string sending a stranger to a flag the harness would refuse is worse than no help string, and it survived a green suite because the usage text was only ever asserted to *contain the verb's name*. Fixed to name the three real values. Recorded because the general lesson is the one M8.11 already paid for: prose that describes an enum has to be checked against the enum, not against itself.
- 2026-09-08 · (M8.14, THE DEFECT ONLY THE OTHER PLATFORM COULD SHOW — and the reason CI is a second observer rather than a formality) **On POSIX, a second harness on one home STOLE the control endpoint from the first** · `start()` removes a leftover socket file so a crashed run does not block the next boot (SDD §10). Removing it unconditionally means the second instance deletes the FIRST one's live socket and binds over it: the first then answers nobody while `ephctl` talks to the second, on a home that already has one book of record and one single committer (EXIT-M8 §4 names two instances as a hazard for exactly that reason). On Windows the OS refuses a duplicate pipe name, so `start()` failed on its own and the hole was invisible on the machine it was written on — the test asserting the degradation was green here and red on linux. Fixed by asking before deciding: the address is probed with a 250 ms connect and a SERVED one is refused with a sentence rather than with `EADDRINUSE`; only then is a leftover cleared, and only on the platform that has one. The probe runs on BOTH platforms deliberately — the first fix put it inside the `win32` branch and two mutations of it survived on win32 because no local test could reach them, which is the general rule: a guard only one platform executes is a guard only one platform's tests can check. Both halves are now tested, and the crashed-harness half is the regression the fix could have caused. **`src/main/hooks.ts` has the identical unconditional `rmSync` and is NOT changed here** — it is M1 code with its own contract, and widening this package into it is a §8 question rather than a fix; recorded so the Architect can decide.
- 2026-09-09 · (ADR-0034, THE MUST-ASK M8.14 LEFT OPEN — and the Architect's answer went further than the report proposed) **Guard AND lock: a second harness on one home hires nobody** · The report proposed mirroring M8.14's endpoint guard in `src/main/hooks.ts` and stopping there. The Architect asked for the boot-level lock as well, and that is the right call on the evidence: a refused endpoint only stops that plane being stolen, while the second instance would still boot, still hire and still commit through a second single committer — which is what actually produced the duplicate `seq` in the Architect's own log on 2026-09-07, and what `EXIT-M8` §4 already warns a reader about. The guard is defence for each plane; the lock is the thing that addresses the recorded damage.
- 2026-09-09 · (ADR-0034, WHERE THE GUARD GOES — one funnel, not two call sites) **`CompanyStart.blockedBy()`, consulted in `startWork`** · `boot()` and `grant()` both pass through `startWork`, so a guard there cannot be forgotten by either — and `ephctl consent:grant` reaches the same class, so the CLI is refused for free with the reason. Deliberately NOT folded into `ConsentVerdict`: consent is a standing answer the Architect gave, occupancy is a condition of this machine right now, and collapsing them would make a busy home read as "nobody has said go". The refusal does NOT set `started`, because the condition clears when the other harness stops and a latched flag would mean the company never came up. And the grant is still PERSISTED: the Architect's answer is their answer; what is refused is starting, which `ConsentGrantOutcome.ok` now reports honestly.
- 2026-09-09 · (ADR-0034, WHY THERE IS NO LOCKFILE) **Liveness is probed from what a live harness already serves** · A pidfile is the conventional answer and it owes a schema and a validator (invariant §9), a stale-lock policy, and a release on every exit route — including the routes that do not run, which is how stale locks are born. Two live sockets answer the same question for free and leave nothing behind after a crash. `control-endpoint.json` is read only to NAME the owner in the refusal; a stale one does not make a home occupied, because refusing to start the company over a leftover file would turn every crash into an outage. The residual is stated in the ADR rather than hidden: a first harness whose endpoints BOTH failed to bind is invisible to the probe, and it is already crippled and saying so through its own degradations.
- 2026-09-09 · (ADR-0034, A MECHANICAL CHOICE, recorded per BUILD-PROMPT §8.2) **`isListening` moved out of `control.ts` into `src/main/home-lock.ts`, which joins the `agora` coverage row** · Three callers now need the probe and none of them should import another subsystem for it. The row is `agora` because what two instances endanger is one book of record and one single committer (invariant §4/§5, ADR-0004) — and because `home` is 114 executable lines, where a new module would have jumped it ten points and failed as a STALE RECORD. The condition's cause is `agora/home-occupied` for the same reason, and not `home/*`: the `DIAGNOSIS.md` probe table has an `agora` area and no `home` area, so a `home/*` cause would have shown in the conditions list and turned no row. It reads WAITING FOR YOU rather than BROKEN — nothing has gone wrong; the harness refused to let it.
- 2026-09-09 · (ADR-0034, AN OBSERVATION FROM PROVING IT — flagged rather than fixed) **Both instances write `DIAGNOSIS.md`, so the file alternates between two accounts of one home** · The live proof ran two harnesses on one home and worked exactly as designed — B refused, hired nobody, named A's pid, and never took either endpoint. But BOTH write `DIAGNOSIS.md` every sixty seconds, and they disagree honestly: A is a working company and reads `the book of record | WORKING`, while B carries `agora/home-occupied` and would read `WAITING FOR YOU`. Whichever wrote last is what a reader sees, and nothing in the file says which instance produced it. The lock does not remove this because it deliberately lets a blocked instance keep running its window and its diagnostics — which is the right call for the person sitting at B. The minimal fix is probably that a blocked instance should not write the SHARED report of a home it does not own (its own condition is in its own window and on its console), but that is a judgement about who `DIAGNOSIS.md` is for, and it is the Architect's rather than mine. Recorded rather than taken, the same way the `hooks.ts` question was at the M8.14 close — where the Architect's answer went further than the report proposed.
- 2026-09-09 · (ADR-0034 SECOND PASS — ARCHITECT DECISION, and it was bigger than the observation that prompted it) **A blocked instance writes NOTHING into a home it does not own** · The observation filed the day before was narrow: both instances write `DIAGNOSIS.md`, so the shared report alternates between two honest and different accounts. Asked to fix it, the check found the larger half: `degradations.report()` appends every condition to `log.jsonl` through `index.ts`'s `append` closure, so a blocked instance was writing into the OWNER's book of record for its whole life — not one `orchestrator/not-started` row, but every degradation it ever raised. The live proof of the day before had reproduced exactly that at seq 18 and I had read it as evidence the lock worked. The rule is now the simple one the Architect stated: an instance that does not own the home writes nothing into it. Enforced at the two seams that already existed — `DegradationLog`'s single private `append` and `DiagnosisWriter.write()`, which covers boot, the minute timer and the quit path — rather than at each call site, because a rule with one enforcement point cannot be forgotten at a second one. The ring and the window keep every condition: invariant §7 still owes the blocked instance's own user the truth.
- 2026-09-09 · (ADR-0034 SECOND PASS — A TEST DELETED, AND WHY THAT IS THE FIX) **`blockedBy` closes over a boot-time constant, so "does not latch" was a guarantee the product does not make** · `index.ts` computes `occupancy` once and `blockedBy: () => occupancy.occupied ? … : null` closes over it, so nothing can flip it inside a process. The class-level test asserting that a blocked company starts once the other harness stops was therefore green against a path production cannot reach — the `ask-the-question-where-production-asks-it` failure, in a test I had written the same day. The Architect's answer settles the behaviour too: occupancy is a boot-time decision and the refusal says to stop the other harness and restart, because a company that quietly starts itself minutes later is a surprise nobody asked for. The test is deleted with a comment in its place saying why `started` is left false — a block is not a start, not an offer of recovery.
- 2026-09-09 · (ADR-0034 SECOND PASS — PROVENANCE) **`DIAGNOSIS.md` names the process that wrote it** · The same reasoning as M8.13's age line, which is FIRST in the file because a stale report read as current is a degradation failing as good news. A report that cannot say WHO wrote it can be misread the same way, and this whole rule was found by two reports disagreeing. The header now reads ``- home: `<path>` (written by process <pid>)``. Cheap, and useful even on a machine where only one harness ever runs — which is what makes it worth keeping now that a blocked instance writes nothing and, in practice, only the owner ever writes.
- 2026-09-09 · (M8b.1 — ARCHITECT DECISION) **The profile bundle's playbooks are COPIED into an instance-scoped directory in the home at activation, not resolved from the repository at spawn** · Finding 8's seam had two candidate designs and the obvious one is forbidden: `<home>/profiles/` is the Architect's override root and `ProfileStore` resolves it BEFORE the built-ins, so seeding a built-in there would do exactly what that store's doc comment refuses — "a silently seeded copy would shadow the built-in forever, so the next Ephesus that shipped a corrected Skeleton Crew would not be the one running." The copy therefore lands under `<home>/instances/<dir>/playbooks/`: invisible to profile resolution, keyed on the ACTIVATION rather than the profile name so two targets running one profile cannot overwrite each other's runbooks. Copying at all is the same rule the plan already obeys — restored verbatim, drift disclosed via `profileVersion` rather than silently applied — and it makes the home self-contained, so a moved or deleted Ephesus checkout can no longer disarm a running company.
- 2026-09-09 · (M8b.1 — ARCHITECT DECISION) **A failed playbook install REFUSES the activation, and every restored instance is re-audited at boot** · Unlike `beforeHires`, which ADR-0021 says cannot refuse because a company still works without an engine-trust record. An instance whose hires cannot read the runbook they are told to follow cannot do the one thing it was activated for, and `profile.ts` already refuses a bundle whose trigger names a playbook it does not carry — this is that rule one boundary further on. The refusal sits before the first spawn, so it costs nothing: on 2026-09-09 the alternative cost four hires, eighteen incidents, a duty re-firing every fifteen minutes and $11.22. The boot audit covers what an activation-time check structurally cannot — `activations.json` survives a restart and the home directory it points into might not — and reports `profiles/playbooks-missing` naming the instance, the files, the directory and the remedy.
- 2026-09-09 · (M8b.1 — WHAT THE ADVERSARIAL PASS FOUND, and the reason the pass is not optional) **Installing the runbook was not enough: the engine could not read it** · After 27 green tests and 15 killed mutants, the question "the file is on disk and the message names it — can the agent actually OPEN it?" answered no. `<home>/instances/…/playbooks/` sits outside the directory an agent is spawned in, which is exactly where `agora/agents/<id>/` sits — and `mailboxPermissions` exists because the engine's permission model blocks that position, discovered when a real agent in the M2 exit demo answered "the write was blocked by permissions". Without a grant the agent meets a permission prompt nobody may answer during an unattended hour (Finding 10) or reports it cannot read its runbook: Finding 8 again, one step along, with the file present. Fixed with a READ-ONLY grant — narrower than the mailbox's `Read`+`Edit` on purpose, because a runbook is the standard the work is judged against and an agent that could rewrite it could lower the bar it is held to. **The generalisation: a file the harness writes into the home for an agent to read is not delivered until the engine is permitted to read it, and no test of the writer can tell you that.**
- 2026-09-09 · (M8b.1 — A MUTANT THAT SURVIVED, AND THE TEST IT WAS MISSING) **Two correct halves and no test of the join** · Round 2 planted `playbooksDir: null` in `AgentManager.spawnConfig` and every test stayed green: `playbookPermissions` was tested against a config that already carried the directory, and `instanceFor` was tested for answering during a spawn, but nothing exercised the line joining them. The state was reachable from outside — spawn through a real `AgentManager` with a real `ClaudeAdapter` and read the settings file the spawn writes — so it was a missing test rather than an equivalent mutant. Worth recording because the same gap exists today for `toolsFor`, which reaches `spawnConfig` by the identical route and has no join test either.
- 2026-09-09 · (M8b.1 — A DEFECT FOUND BY WRITING THE TEST, not by review) **`instanceDirName` left `.` in its safe set, so `..` folded to itself** · The function claims to be total over strings the id schema cannot produce; `'..'` would have named the parent of `instances/`. A defence-in-depth fold that passes through the one string the filesystem treats as an instruction is not defence in depth. `.` removed from the safe set, the empty fold mapped to `-`, and the property asserted over a hostile list rather than a happy-path example.
- 2026-09-09 · (M8b.3 — A FILED FINDING REFUTED BY ITS OWN EVIDENCE, and the correction is the package) **The brief WAS written; the `briefRef` resolves against the agora root, not the home** · Finding 13 reported "the one brief in the run was archived pointing at a file that was never written", severity high, on the strength of `ls $EPH_HOME/odeon/briefs/… → No such file or directory` and `there is no odeon/ directory at all`. `fileBrief` writes under `agoraRoot` — `<home>/agora` — and logs `briefRef` RELATIVE TO THAT. The file is at `C:\Users\senso\ephrun\agora\odeon\briefs\2026-09-09T06-08-00-374Z.md`, 1,781 bytes, exactly the name in the row; the runner's own `ls $EPH_HOME` output lists `agora/` and they never looked inside it. The filed acceptance ("archiving is atomic with writing") was already satisfied: the write precedes the log row and refuses rather than overwriting. **The defect is resolution, not atomicity** — a path whose root is stated nowhere, in a field named `briefRef`, read by a person who has only the home. `briefRef` and `minutesRef` are WRITE-ONLY (nothing in `src/` reads either back; the BRIEFS tab lists the directory), so the Architect's decision is to make them home-relative; `deckRef` is left alone because the ledger does read it back. The general lesson is the one the log already carries twice: absence in one vocabulary is not absence, and a finding graded high on a negative `ls` deserves the second place to look before it is filed.
- 2026-09-09 · (M8b.2 — ARCHITECT DECISION) **A declined floor ends a round; a full round of declines adjourns the meeting; and `odeon:adjourn` joins the control surface** · Artemis offered two fixes for the meeting that could not end and said either would do — "adjourn when the only attendee yields, or treat a declined floor as ending the round". The second was chosen because the first is a special case of it: nothing could end a meeting of ANY size, `after()` wraps for two attendees exactly as it does for one, and a one-attendee-only fix leaves the defect one step away for the next runner who convenes two. `declinedInARow` counts declines since the last thing said; anything said, and any Architect interjection, resets it. The verb was added because `odeon:convene` shipped with no counterpart and, exactly like Finding 3's `budget:set`, was not in the deliberately-refused list either — a CLI runner could open a meeting and had no way to close one.
- 2026-09-09 · (M8b.2 — THE ROUTE THE FINDING DID NOT NAME) **`refuse` was ACCEPTED by the odeon endpoint and never HANDLED, so the decline was recorded as an aside and the driver never heard it** · `ENDPOINT_CONTRACTS` gave the odeon `accepts: ['propose','inform','agree','refuse','done']` and `handles: ['propose','inform']`. Hermes logs an accepted-but-unhandled act as `aside: true` and skips it — which is why seq 442 shows Artemis's refusal delivered and nothing happening. Finding 11 concluded the meeting could not terminate; it also could not HEAR that anyone had yielded, and the two are separate defects with separate fixes. `refuse` is now handled, and the dispatch splits it: from the floor-holder it is a decline, from anyone else it is answered as the aside it always was and is NEVER handed to the filing parser — because "I cannot do that" is not a malformed deck, which is the regression the aside mechanism exists to prevent.
- 2026-09-09 · (M8b.2 — A MUTATION ROUND THAT REPORTED A SCORE IT HAD NOT EARNED, caught by its own control) **A non-zero exit is not a failing test: the suite's RAM guard refuses to start and exits 1** · the M8b.2 round came back 11/12 with the planted no-op control KILLED, which is impossible if the harness is sound. Free memory had fallen to 0.90 GB and `test/global-setup.ts`'s `requireHeadroom` refuses below 1 GB — exiting non-zero without running a single test, which the harness scored as a kill. Every verdict in that round was therefore unearned, including the eleven that looked right. The harness now distinguishes "the suite ran and went red" from "the suite refused to start" and reports the latter as INVALID. **This is the whole argument for planting a no-op control in every mutation round**: rounds 1 and 2 of M8b.1 are trustworthy precisely because THEIR controls survived, which a refusing suite could not have produced. A mutation score with no control is a number with no condition.
- 2026-09-09 · (M8b.3 — A RED BASELINE MAKES EVERY MUTANT LOOK KILLED) **A mutation round must prove the suite is GREEN before it mutates anything** · the first M8b.3 round reported 7/7 with the planted control killed. The harness was sound this time: `test/main/org.test.ts` was ALREADY FAILING on the clean tree, because its assertion resolved the retro ref against the agora root — the very assumption the package changes. With a red baseline every mutant is trivially "killed", the no-op included. The harness now runs the suite once before the first mutant and aborts unless it is green, which is the general fix for this and for the earlier RAM refusal: both were the harness reading "non-zero exit" as "the mutant did it". That failing test is also the best evidence the change was load-bearing — it was the only place in the tree that actually RESOLVED one of these refs.
- 2026-09-09 · (M8b.3 — A MUTATION TEST SET NARROWER THAN THE AFFECTED SET) **Six killed mutants, and two scenario tests still broke** · the round ran four unit files and every mutant died; the full suite then failed `s-meeting.test.ts` twice, because those scenarios resolve `closed.ref` against `eph.agora.root` and the round never loaded them. A mutation score is scoped to the files the round runs, and reporting "6/6 killed" without saying WHICH tests were running is the same defect as a coverage figure with no platform: a number without its condition. The Definition of Done caught it, which is why mutation is evidence ON TOP of the suite rather than instead of it.
- 2026-09-09 · (M8b.3 — A HAZARD OF THIS CHECKOUT, worth knowing before the next long run) **A source file reverted to a stale version mid-round, six mutants after it had been restored and verified** · during the M8b.3 round `src/shared/odeon.ts` came back carrying mutant P1's text — the round's FIRST mutant, restored and hash-verified six mutants earlier, with P2 and P3 having since passed their own pre- and post-checks on that same file. Nothing in the harness could have written it. The working tree lives under `C:\Users\senso\OneDrive`, and a stale write landing late is the signature of a sync client replaying an older version of a rapidly-rewritten file. It also explains tree damage earlier in the session that had been attributed to a killed process. The practical rules: a baseline hash catches it, `git status` after every round catches it, and a package should be COMMITTED before its mutation round so `git checkout --` is the restore rather than a write the harness has to trust.
- 2026-09-10 · (M8c.9 — ARCHITECT DECISION, taken with the root cause in hand rather than from the two options as filed) **A surviving worktree is REUSED where it stands, and ownership is the repository link rather than the branch name** · The filing offered reuse or replacement and named their failure modes. Settling which of the two runs was stale changed the question: neither is: `activate` still takes over a `down` instance exactly as the exit run recorded, and the refusal is one layer down in `Worktrees.create`, which required the surviving checkout to be sitting on `agent/<id>` or `agent/<id>-<topic>`. The exit run's crew never left that branch because Finding 8 left it with no runbook; the rehearsal's crew cut branches, reproduced a failure at a commit and opened three pull requests, and ended the hour somewhere else. **The finding was invisible for exactly as long as the crew was unable to work.** Reuse was chosen because `--force` appears nowhere in this module by design and the rehearsal's crew had unpushed work in that position. What is given up is nothing the refusal protected: `git-common-dir` was always the test that stops an agent being handed a stranger's repository, and the test file said so in as many words while the code beside it required the branch to match anyway.
- 2026-09-10 · (M8c.9 — A DEFECT FOUND WHILE SETTLING WHICH OBSERVATION WAS STALE, verified by execution) **The shipped runbook asked every hire for a branch name git cannot create** · `profiles/skeleton-crew/playbooks/incident.md` §5, `docs/ENGINEERING-STANDARDS.md` §2 and `CLAUDE.md` all say an agent pushes `agent/<name>/<topic>`. A harness hire is already ON `agent/<name>`, and `git branch agent/mason/fix-geo` then fails: `cannot lock ref 'refs/heads/agent/mason/fix-geo': 'refs/heads/agent/mason' exists`. So every hire that followed its runbook hit a refusal, improvised a name outside the namespace, and the namespace check read the improvisation as somebody else's checkout — the two halves of one defect, one in a runbook and one in `git.ts`. The runbook and the standard now say `agent/<name>-<topic>` with the reason. **OWED TO M7b:** `SRS.md` §257, `SDD.md` §752 and ADR-0019 describe the Recursive Improvement improver pushing the slash form, and that improver will be a harness hire with `agent/<name>` minted, so it meets the same wall. Not edited here — an ADR is append-only and the SRS/SDD are outside this package's acceptance — but it is a real future conflict rather than a stylistic one.
- 2026-09-10 · (M8c.9 — THE OTHER HALF OF THE CLOSED LOOP, and why the fix is not a blanket catch) **`deactivate` does not ask to kill a crew the harness already records as `down`** · The rehearsal met `the harness failed: agents: no agent "…"` from `AgentManager.kill`'s `require`, thrown out of the middle of `deactivate` AFTER the triggers were disarmed and the hires released — so the refusal also left the instance half torn down, which is what made it a loop rather than an inconvenience. A restored instance's crew is `down` by design (ADR-0027) and `ProfileInstance.crew` already records it, so the kills are skipped rather than attempted-and-caught: a `try` round a call that is simply wrong to make would have hidden the error instead of removing it. The `catch` is kept for the LIVE case only, where an agent may genuinely have died since, and what it catches is written to the `deactivated` row as `unkillable` rather than swallowed (invariant §7). The row also carries `crew`, so a reader can tell "four agents stopped" from "four agents were already down" — which the row could not say before.
- 2026-09-10 · (M8c.9 — A DELETION, recorded because deleting tested code deserves a reason) **`isAgentsOwnBranch` removed rather than kept unused** · After ownership became the repository link the predicate had no caller. `check-invariants` walks MODULES, not functions, so an exported, tested, uncalled function passes every mechanical gate — the M6 Herald shape at function scale. Its two behaviours that still matter are now asserted at the seam, as statements about `create`: a topic branch beneath the agent's own is reused, and a foreign repository is refused. The 2026-09-06 incident its doc comment recorded moved into `create`'s comment beside the rehearsal's, so the history is where the decision is.
- 2026-09-10 · (M8c.9 — WHAT THE ADVERSARIAL PASS TRIED AND COULD NOT BREAK, including the one gap it decided not to close) **The join between `activate` and the worktree seam is already proven — by the rehearsal itself** · The M8b.1 lesson says two correct halves and no test of the join is where a mutant hides, and this package has exactly that shape: `ProfileActivations.activate` is tested with a stubbed `spawn`, and `AgentManager.isolate` is tested with real git and real directories, and nothing tests them composed. It was left uncomposed on the evidence rather than on convenience: **the rehearsal is the join test.** The refusal it recorded — `hire "ci-babysitter" could not spawn: … worktree refused: "…" already exists — nothing was activated` — is `create`'s sentence carried verbatim through `isolate`, through `spawn`, through `activate`, to the CLI. The join worked; the rule it carried was wrong. `activate`'s behaviour on a rejecting spawn is separately covered by the `failOn` cases. Building a `ProfileActivations` over a real `AgentManager` would be a large new rig proving a link a live run already demonstrated end to end.
- 2026-09-10 · (M8c.9 — AN OBSERVATION, recorded rather than fixed) **Deactivating a `down` crew leaves its worktrees standing, and that is the design rather than a leak** · A live crew's kill unwinds each spawn, and `releaseWorktree` removes the clean ones; a down crew has nothing to unwind, so `<home>/worktrees/agent.*` survives a restart-then-deactivate with whatever the agents left in it. Removing them would be the "replace" option the Architect did not choose, and `remove` refuses a dirty worktree anyway. Nothing is blocked by it — the widened `create` reuses exactly those directories on the next activation, which is the point. Worth knowing because a home that has been restarted and deactivated several times will hold worktrees for agents that are not coming back until the Architect activates that instance again.
- 2026-09-10 · (M8c.9 — A PRE-EXISTING EDGE THE WIDENING DOES NOT INTRODUCE, tried and left alone) **A repository path that differs only in case would be read as a different repository** · `path.resolve(target, common).startsWith(repo + path.sep)` is case-sensitive, and `git rev-parse --git-common-dir` returns the path in whatever case git recorded at `worktree add` — which came from the same `plan.repo` both times, so the comparison matches in every path the shipped app takes. An Architect who reactivates against `c:\…\repo` having activated against `C:\…\repo` would get a refusal naming the wrong cause. This behaved identically before the change (`owned` required the same test), so it is neither a regression nor in this package's acceptance; normalising path comparison is a change with its own blast radius and it is recorded here rather than taken in a fix package.
- 2026-09-10 · (M8c.8 — ARCHITECT DECISION, recorded as ADR-0035) **An engine's permission prompt is answered in advance, per hire, by name — not by the harness answering it** · The rehearsal settled what the alternative costs: twelve prompts in the hour and four of five agents ending on one, so the hour lasts until the first agent reaches a prompt rather than until the work ends. Option 2 (escalate it as a real Ephesus gate the harness then answers on the PTY) was refused for what it IS rather than for difficulty: a harness that can type into a permission dialog can approve anything the engine would have asked about, in advance of knowing what that is, which is the authority ADR-0033 refuses to a script. Option 3 (`bypassPermissions` at `autonomous`) reverses ADR-0031 on that ADR's own reasoning. Declaring beforehand is a smaller power and is auditable before the fact; anything undeclared still parks, which is what keeps it least privilege rather than a bypass.
- 2026-09-10 · (M8c.8 — A SEAM THE COVERAGE FLOOR REFUSED, and it was right about the DESIGN rather than the number) **The declaration rides on the spawn request, not through an `unattendedFor` option** · The first version mirrored `toolsFor`: an option on `AgentManager`, wired in `index.ts`. `check-coverage` failed the `boot` row for one more uncovered arrow in `index.ts` — which is exactly what that row measures. The right reading was not "lower the floor" but "why is there a seam at all": `toolsFor` exists because tool directories must be RESOLVED against a target and checked for containment, a filesystem concern that can fail and must be reported. A command grant is text a bundle wrote and the adapter renders. So it rides beside `envGrants` on the spawn request that already carries the rest of the hire's declaration, and both the seam and the `index.ts` line disappeared. A floor that fails on a wiring line is doing the job GYM-006 gave it.
- 2026-09-10 · (M8c.8 — WHAT THE ADVERSARIAL PASS BROKE, twice, after 14 green tests and a clean mutation round) **A shared bundle could have granted itself unprompted commands, and `tools` had the same hole since M8.7b** · `inspectImport`'s widening check is the answer to FR-10.4's sharpest attack — a bundle reusing a trusted name arriving with more authority — and it covered `envGrants` and `autonomy` only. ADR-0035 creates a new authority, so a bundle could have arrived carrying `curl … | sh` as an unprompted command with nothing to say so. The same look found that `tools` (directories an agent reads as INSTRUCTIONS, ADR-0026's entire subject) had never been covered there either. Both closed together, because a manifest that disclosed one and not the other would look complete and not be. **The second break: `git push -u origin agent/` as a prefix grant is not bounded by the branch namespace** — git's refspec is `<src>:<dst>`, so `git push -u origin agent/x:main` starts with that prefix and pushes to main. The shipped grants are now the exact `HEAD` forms, which cannot name a destination at all. The general rule, now in the ADR: use the exact form wherever an argument could change WHERE a command acts rather than HOW.
- 2026-09-10 · (M8c.8 — A RESIDUAL PINNED BY A TEST RATHER THAN HIDDEN) **A grant the bundle has since removed survives in a settings file the harness did not replace** · Nothing distinguishes a `Bash(...)` rule this harness wrote from one the Architect wrote, so the dedupe set can stop a respawn ACCUMULATING its own grants but cannot retract one the declaration no longer contains. It bites hardest in a reused worktree (M8c.9), where `settings.local.json` outlives the spawn; the normal path restores the Architect's backed-up file, so it needs a crash to persist. A permission that outlives its declaration is the wrong direction to be wrong in, so it is a named test and an ADR consequence rather than a comment. Closing it needs a discriminator the settings format does not offer.
- 2026-09-10 · (M8c.1 — ARCHITECT DECISION) **`budget:set` exists, and it may TIGHTEN and never raise** · The acceptance allowed a verb or a refusal. A refusal would have left `EXIT-M8` §2 marking a window-only step mandatory for a runner the whole script assumes has no mouse, so the script needed changing either way — and it would have refused something that authorises nothing.
